### What actually gets a driver — the inventory

#### "Device X got no driver" — walk the chain in this order

The single most-asked field question, and the answer is spread across four subsystems, so guessing wastes a whole session (it cost three in a row before this table existed). Every step below names the **log line that settles it**, so it is answerable from a pasted log alone. Stop at the first step that explains the device — do not change any list until you know which layer dropped it.

| # | Question | Log line that answers it | If this is the culprit |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Was the device even in the scan list? | `[PNP] N eszköz a szűrés után…` + `[PNP] N eszköz kizárva a driver-keresésből (nincs PNPDeviceID=…, virtuális gép eszköze=…, osztály=…)`, names at DEBUG under `[PNP] Kizárva (<ok>)` | `_filter_wu_scan_devices` / `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES` / `_VIRTUAL_DEVICE_NAME_RE` |
| 2 | Was it merged into an identical sibling? | `[PNP] … azonos HWID-ű példányok összevonva: [(név, db)]` | By design — the kept twin carries `dup_count`; a *different* device with the same first HWID would be the bug |
| 3 | Is it a storage or firmware device? | `[AUTOFIX-WU] … kihagyva a WU-egyeztetésből` for the WU path, `[AUTOFIX-CAT] … kihagyva a katalógus-zárókörből` for the Catalog rounds | **Permanently excluded since 2026-09-02** — no checkbox, no flag, no way to enable (see [Tároló és firmware](#tároló-és-firmware-véglegesen-tiltva-nincs-hozzá-kapcsoló-2026-09-02)). Not a bug, and **not** something to "fix" by re-adding a switch. Besides step 1, this is the ONLY device-level exclusion AutoFix has. (Until 2026-09-02 it was a pair of default-off checkboxes, so an older log may read as if the tech left them unticked.) |
| 4 | Did WU return anything at all? | `[WU_API] Talált frissítések: N` and `[AUTOFIX-WU] A(z) K. kör WU keresése lefutott: N nyers találat` — or `A WU keresés elbukott` | A failed search is **not** "everything installed"; the catalog round is the fallback |
| 5 | Did a WU package pair with this device? | the device simply never appears in `✅ Telepítendő driverek száma` | HWID pairing — `_hwid_matches` (bus + token subset, ≥2 tokens). This is the R9-200 class of bug |
| 6 | Was a matched package then filtered out? | `[KIHAGYVA] Downgrade-védelem: …` / `[AUTOFIX] Régebbi verzió kihagyva: …` (DEBUG) / `[AUTOFIX-WU] Firmware-csomagok kihagyva` / `Véglegesen kizárt (telepítés-hiba…)` | `_filter_wu_downgrades`, `_filter_wu_older_duplicates`, `filter_firmware_updates`, `wu_failed_uids` |
| 7 | Did the Catalog round even ask about it? | `[CATALOG] Mély szken köre: N/M eszköz (kizárva: osztály=…)`, names at DEBUG | **Only `DEEP_CATALOG_BLOCKED_CLASSES` can drop a device here as of 2026-07-28** — i.e. only the two checkboxes. `_VENDOR_HWID_RE` and `GENERIC_REPLACE_BLOCKED_INFS` are **deleted**; if you are reading an older log, `nincs gyártói HWID=…` / `busz-INF=…` counts in this line came from them |
| 7b | Did it have a usable search key? | `[CATALOG] Kihagyva (csak típuskódos azonosítói vannak, azokra bármely gyártó csomagja illeszkedne): <név> [id-k]` (DEBUG) | `is_specific_hwid`. The device *was* in the round, but every one of its IDs is a type code (`ACPI\PNP0501`, `USB\ROOT_HUB30`, `*PNP…`). Querying those returns other vendors' packages, so we don't. **This is the only remaining non-checkbox reason a device gets no Catalog query** |
| 8 | Did the Catalog have rows, and which won? | `[CATALOG] Keresés: <név> (<hwid>)` per HWID, then `[CATALOG] Döntés: … NYERTES: '…' [dátum]` (a sor a közeli versenytársakat is felsorolja) | No `Döntés` line ⇒ zero rows for every HWID tried (incl. the synthesized base ID). **If the winner is from a vendor unrelated to the machine** (field pattern: an `AlpsAlpine` package on an Intel chipset device), suspect a type-code key that slipped past `is_specific_hwid` — read the `Keresés:` lines to see which ID produced it, and fix the key test, *not* the device set |
| 9 | Was the winning row rejected? | `[CATALOG] Kihagyva (nem újabb kiadás …)` / `Kihagyva (Windows-alapdriveren fut, nem gyári-csere jelölt…)` / `Dátum szerint ÚJABB, verzió szerint nem - a dátum dönt` | `is_newer_release` (date first) and the inbox guard. The AMD-SMBus-stuck-on-2017 case lived here |
| 10 | Was the package vetoed before install? | `[INF] A csomag egyik INF-je sem illeszkedik az eszközre…` or `[INF] A csomag INF-jei NEM az eszköz buszáról valók…` | `inf_package_applies`. The second line means we failed to *read* the right INF — never a veto |
| 11 | Did it install but not bind? | `[CATALOG_INSTALL] A csomag felment, de az eszköz NEM vette át: …` then `[AUTOFIX] N nem-kötő katalógus-csomag feljegyezve a következő lábnak` | Windows preferred a more specific HWID. Counted as skipped, reported by `_emit_catalog_no_bind` |
| 12 | Nothing above fired? | closing summary: `_emit_driver_health` ("Windows-alapdriveren maradt"), `_emit_missing_packages`, `_emit_catalog_no_bind` | No source has a package. Vendor card / board vendor's page is the remedy |

Two shortcuts worth remembering: a device missing from step 1 can never be fixed by touching a Catalog blocklist (that mistake cost the monitor its drivers for a long time), and steps 7–11 only ever concern the **Catalog** — the WU Agent has no class filter above step 3.

**Read this first, because the obvious mental model is wrong in one important way:** it is *not* "the WU Agent sees everything, and only the Catalog has blocklists". Before either source runs, `_filter_wu_scan_devices` drops whole device classes (`WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`) — and that filter gates **both** paths, so a class listed there is unreachable by the WU Agent too, not just by the Catalog. That is why the monitor got nothing for so long: it was never in the device list to begin with, so no Catalog blocklist change could ever have fixed it. Layer order: (1) `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES` — applies to everything; (2) WU Agent — no further class filter on top; (3) the three Catalog entry points, which **since 2026-07-28 share one rule set**: all three take every device, minus the two checkbox-gated risk classes. When a device "gets no driver", find out which layer dropped it before changing anything — the log names every one of them.

> **THE GOVERNING RULE (explicit user decision, 2026-07-28 — this outranks every list below).** A device is excluded from the driver search in **exactly two** cases, and no third may be added:
> 1. **storage or firmware** — **2026-09-02 óta ez már nem kapcsoló, hanem RÖGZÍTETT SZABÁLY**, lásd [Tároló és firmware: véglegesen tiltva](#tároló-és-firmware-véglegesen-tiltva-nincs-hozzá-kapcsoló-2026-09-02). Korábban két, alapból kikapcsolt jelölőnégyzet volt rá (`mc-allow-storage`/`mc-allow-firmware`, illetve `hw-allow-storage`/`hw-allow-firmware`); azok eltűntek, a szűrők maguk változatlanul működnek;
> 2. **it is not hardware** — the seven conceptual classes in `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES` (volume objects, audio endpoints, the print queue, the HAL, non-PnP entries), plus devices with no `PNPDeviceID` and hypervisor guest devices.
>
> Everything else gets searched, in every round, on every source. There is **one** additional rule, and it is deliberately *not* a device filter: `is_specific_hwid` refuses to use a **type code** as a Catalog search key, because the Catalog answers a type code with any vendor's package for that type. The device still enters the round — only the useless key is skipped.
>
> The three earlier generations of this rule (per-round class whitelists, class blocklists, the vendor-HWID requirement, the bus-enumerator INF list) are all gone, and every one of them turned out to hide real drivers: the class lists kept a printer/keyboard/USB controller from ever being asked about, and the bus-INF list hid `Intel - System 11.7.0.1019` on a `machine.inf` node and `Intel - System 9.21` on the HD Audio controller. **If a round ever needs to be made cheaper, add threads or a cache — never a device filter.**

Four independent sources feed drivers onto a machine, and it is easy to misattribute a gap to the wrong one. **Both the manual scan and AutoFix use the same functions for all of it** — the only difference is that the manual scan lists candidates with checkboxes and AutoFix installs them unattended. Never fork a second implementation for one of the two.

| Source | Covers | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|
| **WU Agent** (`_search_wu_api` + `_match_wu_updates_to_devices`) | every present device, **no class whitelist** — chipset, GPU, audio, LAN, Bluetooth, mice, controllers, cameras | the main path; AutoFix installs automatically, manual scan lists |
| **Update Catalog — error-coded devices** (`_catalog_search_collect`) | devices with `ConfigManagerErrorCode` ≠ 0 that WU had nothing for | automatic in both paths |
| **Update Catalog — generic-driver devices** (`mark_generic_replace_candidates`) | devices running on an inbox driver, no class filter since 2026-07-28 | manual scan: pre-selected with a badge; AutoFix: closing round |
| **Update Catalog — deep scan** (`deep_catalog_candidates`, 2026-07) | **every** remaining device with a vendor-coded HWID — i.e. a device happily running an *old vendor* driver, which none of the rows above reach | manual scan: `start_hw_scan(deep=True)`, default on, checkbox `hw-deep-scan`; AutoFix: `AUTOFIX_DEEP_CATALOG` |
| ~~**Vendor sites (GPU)**~~ | ~~NVIDIA/AMD/Intel~~ | **TÖRÖLVE 2026-09-02** (explicit user decision) — lásd lentebb. A gép/alaplap-gyártói link-kártya (`oemdrivers.py`) MARAD, az nem videokártya. |
| ~~**OEM vendor catalog**~~ | ~~Lenovo/Dell/HP~~ | **TÖRÖLVE 2026-09-02** (explicit user decision) — a modulok sincsenek meg; a gépazonosítás `app/machine_core.py`-ban él tovább |

**Storage and firmware devices are PERMANENTLY excluded in BOTH paths — no checkbox, no flag, no way to enable them** (2026-09-02, explicit user decision; see [Tároló és firmware: véglegesen tiltva](#tároló-és-firmware-véglegesen-tiltva-nincs-hozzá-kapcsoló-2026-09-02)). Until then it was two default-off checkboxes per path (`mc-allow-storage`/`mc-allow-firmware`, `hw-allow-storage`/`hw-allow-firmware`).

**The deep scan (2026-07) is what closes the "old but working vendor driver" hole.** Before it, a device that was neither error-coded nor on an inbox driver was never asked about in the Catalog at all — so a 2023 chipset driver that worked fine simply never updated (WU considers the device healthy and offers nothing). `deep_catalog_candidates` feeds every remaining device to the same `_catalog_search_collect`, and the release gate in `_catalog_find_driver` (strictly-newer only, **by date** since 2026-07-27 — see `is_newer_release`) is what keeps it from downgrading anything. Measured live (2026-07-25, X570 + RTX 3060): 99 present devices → **16** deep candidates → 12s → 3 genuinely newer packages that the old code missed (AMD PCI 1.0.0.83→1.0.0.90, AMD PSP 5.17→5.44, plus the Realtek generic-replace), while correctly rejecting the NVIDIA row (catalog 32.0.15.9595 < installed 32.0.16.1074) and the identical-version Realtek NIC. **Exactly one filter is left: `DEEP_CATALOG_BLOCKED_CLASSES` (the two checkbox-gated risk classes).** The vendor-coded-HWID requirement and the bus-enumerator INF blocklist were removed on 2026-07-28 together with round 3's copies — **both constants (`_VENDOR_HWID_RE`, `GENERIC_REPLACE_BLOCKED_INFS`) are deleted**; a comment block marks their old spot so they don't get reintroduced as device filters. Measured immediately after, on the same Intel Q170 box: **95 devices → 91 in the combined catalog round, and the only 4 left out were the 3 storage devices and 1 firmware device the checkboxes gate** — i.e. the device set is now exactly "everything except the user's two exceptions". Cost: the full round went to **48s on 10 threads** (91 devices × up to 4 HWID queries each). Gain on that machine: 4 hits → **10**, including an Intel I219 NIC package, Intel HD Graphics 530 `31.0.101.x`, the Intel chipset `10.1.1.44`, Intel AMT Ports and the Management Engine Interface — none of which any earlier configuration would have found. One of the ten (`AlpsAlpine - System` on an Intel chipset device) looks like an OEM bundle indexed under a shared chip ID, the same shape as the Clevo-Realtek case; `inf_package_applies` plus the post-install bind check are what catch those, at the cost of a download. **`DEEP_CATALOG_BLOCKED_CLASSES` used to be deliberately narrower than the generic-replace blocklist** (HID/Display/Ports allowed, on the theory that updating an existing vendor driver to its own newer version is not the same risk as swapping Microsoft's driver for a vendor one) — **as of 2026-07-28 the two are identical** (`STORAGE_RISK_CLASSES | FIRMWARE_RISK_CLASSES`), because that distinction produced the indefensible result that a device got no better driver *because* it was on a worse one. **Storage must stay in it**: during development the deep set did pull in `Standard NVM Express Controller` and `Standard SATA AHCI Controller` before that list existed, which is exactly the `INACCESSIBLE_BOOT_DEVICE` scenario the whole project avoids.

**The list below is now mostly HISTORY — read it as the reasoning behind the two surviving exclusions, not as a live blocklist.** As of 2026-07-28 only storage and firmware are held back, both behind checkboxes, and both fully available once ticked. Every other entry that used to live here (Display, Monitor, Processor, Battery, Printer, CDROM, WPD, USB, HID/keyboard/mouse, PCI bridges) has been reversed, each after the same discovery: the exclusion was hiding a real driver. The reasons are kept because they explain *why* storage and firmware are different in kind — and because each reversal is a warning about how easy it is to write a plausible-sounding ban that costs the shop real drivers. **Do not add a new entry here**; see the governing rule above.

- **Storage controllers** (`SCSIAdapter`, `HDC`) — **from the Catalog, in AutoFix, always; from WU in AutoFix, unless the tech ticks the box**: a bad controller swap surfaces as `INACCESSIBLE_BOOT_DEVICE` at the *next* boot, where no rollback of ours can run — not even the restore point, only recovery media. **The rule used to be enforced on the Catalog side only** (2026-07-27 audit): `SCSIAdapter`/`HDC` are not in `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`, so they sailed through to `_match_wu_updates_to_devices` and AutoFix would have installed a WU-offered NVMe/AHCI driver unattended — the code and this document disagreed, and it stayed hidden only because WU happened to offer nothing for the field machine's controller. Resolved by **explicit user decision**: not a silent ban (WU packages are WHQL and the server itself vets applicability, which is a different risk class from a scraped Catalog row) but a second checkbox on the AutoFix confirm dialog — `mc-allow-storage`, **default off, and forced back off every time the dialog opens** because it is a per-machine, boot-critical judgement, not a remembered setting. `wu_core.filter_autofix_risky_devices` applies it (together with the firmware switch) to the WU device list and names every dropped device in the log. The choice rides the chain through `--allow-storage-drivers` exactly like the printer flag (both appended by `_schedule_autofix_resume`, never by call sites). **Firmware has its own, second checkbox** (`mc-allow-firmware`, also default off and reset on every open, explicit user decision 2026-07-27). It is deliberately *not* folded into the storage switch, because the failure mode is a category worse: a firmware update rewrites non-volatile memory (UEFI/BIOS, SSD controller, TPM, dock), no restore point or Windows reinstall can undo it, an interrupted flash kills the device in hardware, and a TPM flash can invalidate BitLocker keys. This one closed a real hole rather than a theoretical one: `Firmware` was never in `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`, so **AutoFix has always been able to flash firmware unattended** whenever WU offered it — the Catalog-side block was cosmetic at the time, because firmware devices failed the then-existing vendor-HWID test and `c_firmware.inf` was on the then-existing bus-INF list. **Both of those safety-by-accident mechanisms are gone since 2026-07-28**, so the `Firmware`-class gate in `filter_autofix_risky_devices` is now the *only* thing holding firmware devices back — do not weaken it on the assumption that something else catches them. The gate is enforced at **two levels**, and both are needed: `filter_autofix_risky_devices` drops `Firmware`-class devices, and `filter_firmware_updates` drops firmware *packages* (`is_firmware_update`: `DriverClass == Firmware`, or `\bfirmware\b` in the title/model) — because an SSD firmware package pairs with the *storage controller* and a dock firmware with a *USB* device, so the device-class filter alone would miss them. The title fallback is deliberately generous and every skipped package is logged by name, so over-filtering stays visible in the field log. **The checkbox governs BOTH AutoFix sources** — the WU device list (`filter_autofix_risky_devices`) *and* the Catalog deep scan (`deep_catalog_candidates(include_risky=…)`). It was WU-only for one iteration; that was rejected as confusing (explicit user decision): the tech makes one decision about storage drivers, not one per source. The gain over Microsoft's inbox NVMe/AHCI driver is ~nil on modern hardware; and this project already has a field case of a vendor storage driver (Intel RST `iastorhsa_ext.inf`) wedging the PnP stack for ~143s per package. **[VISSZAVONVA 2026-09-02 — a két kapcsoló TELJESEN ELTŰNT mindkét felületről, lásd [Tároló és firmware](#tároló-és-firmware-véglegesen-tiltva-nincs-hozzá-kapcsoló-2026-09-02). Az alábbi bekezdés a *szűrők* működésére továbbra is érvényes — csak az „a technikus bekapcsolhatja" rész nem igaz többé.]** ~~THE MANUAL SCAN HAS THE SAME TWO CHECKBOXES SINCE 2026-08-28, AND THEY ARE ALSO DEFAULT-OFF~~ (explicit user decision: *"legyen checkbox… ugye alapbol LEGYEN KIKAPCSOLVA a csuszka ITT IS, de be lehessen kapcsolni ha be akarom"*). `start_hw_scan(deep, allow_storage, allow_firmware)`; UI: `hw-allow-storage` / `hw-allow-firmware`, red-bordered pills under the deep-scan checkbox, **reset to off on every entry into the view** — same argument as the AutoFix dialog: a per-machine boot-critical judgement must not be a remembered setting, or a forgotten tick installs a storage package on the *next* machine. **This reverses the 2026-07-27 rule** (which was: the manual scan always passes `include_risky=True` and merely renders hits with a red `⛔ KOCKÁZATOS` badge, unticked) — the badge behaviour is unchanged, but now the devices only enter the scan at all when the tech asks. Two implementation rules carried over from the AutoFix side, both load-bearing: the gate is `filter_autofix_risky_devices` applied **once, to the whole device list**, right after `_filter_wu_scan_devices` and before any of the three catalog sources touch it (the branch-level version is exactly the hole that let a single error code bypass both checkboxes); and the **package-level** firmware check is separate and still needed, because an SSD firmware pairs with the *storage controller* and a dock firmware with a *USB* device — `matched_hwids` is therefore updated only **after** that check, so a skipped firmware package cannot also cost the device its real driver in the catalog round. ~~The excluded counts are surfaced **on screen** (`skipped_risky` in `hw_scan_result`), not just in the log: otherwise "why did it find no driver for my SSD?" looks like a bug rather than an unticked box.~~ **[VISSZAVONVA 2026-09-03: a `skipped_risky` mező és a képernyő-sor is TÖRÖLVE — explicit user decision. Az érv azért esett el, mert 2026-09-02 óta ez nem „egy be nem pipált doboz", hanem a program rögzített szabálya: nincs mit észrevenni, a szöveg minden szken végén egy meg nem hozott döntést magyarázott. A naplózás változatlan, lásd a [nézet-átépítés szekcióját](#a-driver-keresés-nézet-átépítése--öt-dobozból-egy-lista-2026-09-03).]** **That badge must come from `wu_core.device_risk_marker`/`mark_device_risk`, applied at every entry point into the pool, not just the deep scan** (fixed 2026-07-28): the flag was set inside `deep_catalog_candidates` only, so the *same* NVMe controller rendered red-and-unticked or silent-and-pre-ticked depending on which source happened to find it — the error-coded `leftover` list and every WU match arrived unmarked, and "select all → install" then put a boot-critical driver on with no warning shown. The WU side additionally checks the *package* with `is_firmware_update` for the same reason `filter_firmware_updates` exists (an SSD firmware pairs with the storage controller, a dock firmware with a USB device). The short list label lives in Python (`RISKY_CLASS_LABEL`/`FIRMWARE_CLASS_LABEL` → `risk_label`), because ui.html used to hardcode the storage wording — "a Windows nem indul el" — onto firmware hits too, which is neither true nor the actual danger. Never pass `include_risky=True` from AutoFix except from the dialog's own switch. **The checkbox gate must sit at the TOP of the AutoFix catalog closing round, on the whole device list — not per branch** (fixed 2026-07-28): the round feeds the Catalog from three sources (error-coded + generic-replace + deep scan), the gate lived only on the deep one, and `problem_devs` was a raw `[d for d in devices_now if d.get('err_code')]`. Neither `_catalog_search_collect` nor `_catalog_find_driver` filters by class, so **a single error code was enough to bypass both checkboxes** and have AutoFix install an NVMe/AHCI or firmware package unattended — the one outcome this whole section exists to prevent. `filter_autofix_risky_devices` now runs once on `devices_now` (with `log_tag`/`context` so its log line names the *catalog* round, not the WU one), which also means a future fourth source cannot reintroduce the hole. The same argument applies to the manual scan's `leftover` list — see the risky-badge rule below. **`DiskDrive` is in `STORAGE_RISK_CLASSES`, not in `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`** (moved 2026-07-27; this paragraph claimed the opposite for a while): the disk normally runs on the universal `disk.inf` with no replacement on offer, but rather than ban it outright it rides the storage checkbox like the controllers — measured on a live box, `KINGSTON SA400S37480G [DiskDrive]` is one of the devices the unticked box holds back. What actually helps an SSD is a **firmware** update, which has its own checkbox.
- ~~**Display, from the Catalog**~~ — **reversed.** The old reasoning (the NVIDIA/AMD/Intel cards are a strictly better source, the Catalog is always older) holds only for a machine *already on a vendor GPU driver*, and such a device is not a generic-replace candidate anyway. On a box stuck on the Microsoft Basic Display Adapter the vendor cards deliver nothing in AutoFix (AMD/Intel are link-out only, the NVIDIA check runs in the manual scan alone), so the Catalog was the only source and we were refusing it. Live-measured after the reversal: an Intel HD Graphics 530 picked up `Intel Corporation - Display - 31.0.101.x` from the Catalog. The release gate still prevents a downgrade.
- ~~**Monitor**~~ — **reversed 2026-07-27 (explicit user decision).** The old reasoning ("EDID/colour metadata, not a functional driver") was the wrong test: a vendor monitor INF installs an EDID override *and the factory ICC colour profile*, which is exactly the half that makes `colorprofile_core`'s reset land on a correct default instead of a bare sRGB guess. Monitor is now out of `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES` (so the WU Agent sees it at all — that filter gates **both** sources), out of `DEEP_CATALOG_BLOCKED_CLASSES`, and a normal generic-replace candidate (it was added to the then-existing `GENERIC_REPLACE_CLASSES` whitelist; since that whitelist was removed on 2026-07-28 it simply is not excluded by anything), so "Generic PnP Monitor" → vendor monitor driver goes through with the usual rollback net. `_VENDOR_HWID_RE` had to learn the `MONITOR\ACI27FA` shape too (no `VEN_`/`VID_` token, but still a specific vendor+model). Nothing here can be boot-critical.
- **Processor / Battery** — also removed from `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES` the same day: AMD/Intel chipset bundles do ship processor power-management drivers and OEMs do ship battery/charge-controller drivers, and neither carries boot risk. They cost nothing in the Catalog paths, because `ACPI\…` IDs fail the `_VENDOR_HWID_RE` vendor-HWID test anyway — only the WU Agent path picks them up.
- **`Printer`, `CDROM`, `WPD` were removed from the device filter too, and `DiskDrive` moved under the storage checkbox** (2026-07-27, explicit user decision: "MINDEN kapjon drivert, semmi ne legyen kizárva, kivéve amit checkboxszal nem engedek"). The printer one fixed a genuine asymmetry: the printer checkbox only ever protected drivers from *deletion*, while `Printer` sat in `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`, so with the box unticked AutoFix deleted the printer driver **and could never look for a replacement**. The checkbox keeps its meaning (don't delete the existing one) — its label now says so — but a printer can get a driver in both positions.
- **Two hidden filters in `_filter_wu_scan_devices` were also opened up, and all of them now log what they dropped.** They had excluded devices for four separate reasons while logging *none* of them, so "why did device X get nothing?" was unanswerable from a field log — a direct violation of the log-everything rule this file opens with, hiding in the one function every driver decision starts from. (a) The name test was a raw `"virtual" in name` substring match that threw out **real hardware** — `Intel(R) Virtual Buttons`, the volume/power buttons on most business laptops and 2-in-1s — and is now `_VIRTUAL_DEVICE_NAME_RE`, word-bounded hypervisor names only (`vmware|virtualbox|vbox|qemu|parallels|hyper-v|xen|virtio`). (b) The blanket `ROOT\` prefix skip is gone: most root-enumerated devices are Microsoft software objects that no source ships a package for, but application-installed drivers live there too (ViGEmBus, VPN TAP adapters), and skipping them also kept a broken one off the "Problémás eszközök" list entirely. The Catalog paths drop them anyway on the vendor-HWID test, so the cost is a few extra in-memory comparisons. **If you ever add an exclusion here, log it in the same `dropped` dict** — a silent filter in this function is how three separate exclusions stayed invisible through several rounds of "does it now cover everything?".
- What remains excluded is only what a driver cannot exist for: `Volume`/`VolumeSnapshot` (the volume object, not the disk or controller behind it — both of those are driverized), `Endpoint`/`AudioEndpoint` (`SWD\MMDEVAPI\…` audio *endpoints* — the speaker/mic entries, not the sound card, which is `MEDIA`), `PrintQueue` (`SWD\PRINTENUM\…`, the queue, not the printer), `LegacyDriver` (a non-PnP driver entry with no device behind it), and `Computer` (the HAL — replacing it means the machine does not boot, and nothing ships a package for it anyway).
- **Keyboard/mouse/HID**: vendors ship *software* (Synapse etc.), not drivers; where a real WHQL package exists the WU path already installs it (field-confirmed: Razer `rz00b9dev.inf`).
- ~~**USB controllers/hubs — for the *generic→vendor swap* only**~~ — **no longer excluded anywhere (2026-07-28)**, when the generic-replace class lists were removed. The old argument (Microsoft's driver is the correct one on modern chipsets, and a failed swap takes out the keyboard and mouse — the very input needed to recover) survives only as a caution, not a rule: the rollback net runs unattended in the same process, so it does not need the user's input to revert, and the measurement below applies here too — nearly every USB-class device is already dropped by the bus-INF rule (`usbxhci.inf`/`usbhub3.inf`/`usb.inf`) or the vendor-HWID rule, so the class entry was protecting almost nothing. **In the deep scan `USB` had already stopped being blocked** (2026-07-27): measured against the field device list, every USB-class device was *already* dropped by one of the other two filters — `usbxhci.inf`/`usbhub3.inf`/`usb.inf` by the bus-INF rule, `USB\ROOT_HUB30` by the vendor-HWID rule — so the class entry protected nothing. The single case it did catch was a controller running a genuine **vendor** USB driver (`oemNN.inf`), which is exactly the case that should be updated, and which the inbox-version guard in `_catalog_find_driver` still protects from a generic→vendor swap by the back door. Same finding applies to `FIRMWARE` (`UEFI\RES_{…}`), `PROCESSOR` (`ACPI\AUTHENTICAMD…`) and `COMPUTER` (`COMPUTER\{GUID}`): all three fail the vendor-HWID test on their own, so listing them was documentation of an intent the code never had to enforce. **`DEEP_CATALOG_BLOCKED_CLASSES` is now exactly `STORAGE_RISK_CLASSES | FIRMWARE_RISK_CLASSES`, and it is the *only* filter the deep scan has** — the bus-INF and vendor-HWID rules that this paragraph used to lean on no longer exist. **Do not add a class back here.** The product rule is that the blocklist has exactly two entries: what the user unticks, and what is not hardware (`WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`). If a wrong-vendor package shows up, the cause is a type-code search key, not a missing class ban — check `is_specific_hwid` first.
- ~~**PCI bridges / root ports / ACPI nodes**~~ — **this entry was wrong and is gone (2026-07-28).** The claim ("no vendor driver exists at all") was never measured, only assumed, and it was the justification for `GENERIC_REPLACE_BLOCKED_INFS`. When the filter came off, a `machine.inf` "Motherboard resources" node returned `Intel - System 11.7.0.1019` and the HD Audio *controller* returned `Intel - System 9.21` — real chipset drivers that had been silently unreachable for as long as the list existed. Bus/enumerator devices are now queried like everything else; the ones that genuinely have nothing simply return zero rows, which costs a request and answers the question honestly.
- **Printer, PrintQueue, AudioEndpoint, Battery, Processor, Computer, Volume, CDROM, WPD**: excluded at the device-filter level (`WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`).

**Boot-path protection is the one exception on the *delete* side (`wu_core._collect_boot_path_protection` / `_is_boot_path_protected`, 2026-07-26, used by BOTH AutoFix paths).** The blocklists above only ever governed *installing*; the delete phase had no class filter at all and `drivers_core.delete_driver_package` calls `pnputil /delete-driver … /uninstall /force` — i.e. it removes an in-use, boot-critical storage driver too (CLAUDE.md already documents the Intel RST case surviving only by *stalling*). On plain AHCI/NVMe that is harmless: Microsoft's inbox `storahci`/`stornvme` takes over. **Behind Intel VMD or an RST RAID volume it is not** — the inbox stack may not cover the controller, and the result is `INACCESSIBLE_BOOT_DEVICE` at the next boot, the one failure mode where none of our rollbacks (restore point included) can ever run, because the machine cannot start. So before deletion the code resolves the device chain carrying `%SystemDrive%` (WMI `Win32_LogicalDiskToPartition` → `…DiskDriveToDiskPartition` → `Win32_DiskDrive.PNPDeviceID`, falling back to `Get-Partition`/`Get-Disk`, then walking `DEVPKEY_Device_Parent` up to 12 levels) and protects every third-party package on it, exactly like the printer protection (published **and** original INF name, since a reinstall renumbers `oemNN.inf`). Two rules that must not be softened: (a) this is **targeted, not class-based** — a class blocklist here would gut the "strip everything" design, and measured live (2026-07-26, WD Blue SN580) the chain resolves to `disk.inf` → `stornvme.inf` → `pci.inf` → `acpi.inf` → `hal.inf`, i.e. all inbox, so **zero** packages get protected and the fix behaves exactly as before; (b) if the chain cannot be resolved at all, it **fails safe** to protecting all `BOOT_FALLBACK_PROTECT_CLASSES` (`SCSIAdapter`/`HDC`/`DiskDrive`) rather than falling back to the old unprotected behaviour — an unnecessarily retained storage driver is at worst stale, a wrongly deleted one is a machine that will not boot.

**Asymmetry worth remembering**: the AutoFix *delete* phase removes third-party packages regardless of class (bar the printer opt-out and the boot-path protection above), but the *reinstall* only reaches classes that survive `WU_SCAN_IGNORED_CLASSES`. A deleted **printer** driver therefore never comes back through us — which is exactly why the printer checkbox exists and why leaving it on is the shop-safe default.

### WU + Catalog engine (shared by AutoFix and the manual scan)

- **WU driver scan/install — shared core (`app/wu_core.py`)**: the PnP query+filter (`WU_PNP_QUERY_PS` + `_filter_wu_scan_devices`), WU↔device matching (`_match_wu_updates_to_devices` → `_hwid_matches`: **prefix match OR same-bus token-subset**, plus title↔name fallback, without which SoftwareComponent packages never match. The token rule is not a nicety: Windows composes hardware IDs as `VEN&DEV&SUBSYS&REV`, while WU frequently ships `VEN&DEV&REV` — SUBSYS sits *between* the tokens, so neither string is a prefix of the other. Field-proven (2026-07, Win10 + Radeon R9 200): `pci\ven_1002&dev_6811&rev_00` never matched `PCI\VEN_1002&DEV_6811&SUBSYS_30001682&REV_00`, so AutoFix **deleted the vendor GPU driver and silently never reinstalled it** — the machine finished the "successful" run on the Microsoft Basic Display Adapter, error-code-free, so even the summary looked clean. Class-scoped `&cc_` package IDs were unreachable for the same reason. IDs with no bus prefix — bare `usbmmidd` — stay exact/prefix-only, since anything looser false-positives on them), and the WUA installer PowerShell (`_build_wu_install_ps`) exist in exactly ONE copy, used by all three consumers: GUI manual install (`app/gui/hwscan.py`: `start_hw_scan` pool + `_install_wu_api`, filtering by exact `UpdateID`s with HWID-prefix fallback), GUI AutoFix (`_scan_and_install_wu_sync`), CLI AutoFix (FÁZIS 6, `match_system_devices=True`). **Never copy this logic back into a class** — the duplicated era produced the "AutoFix works, manual install silently broken for a whole release" bug (Build ~192). Baked-in rules: downloads are **synchronous `$DL.Download()`** — never `BeginDownload($null,...)` (null callbacks throw NullReferenceException instantly; that was the Build-192 regression; hang protection is the Python-side cancel `taskkill`); selection filters by exact `UpdateID` because distinct WU packages can share one HWID (field-seen: 3 selected → 4 installed with HWID-only filtering); with no filter args the script installs NOTHING (`EMPTY`). The script emits a line protocol (`INIT/SEARCH/FOUND/SKIP/TOTAL/DLONE/INSTONE/OK/OKRB/FAIL/EMPTY/DONE/ERROR`) parsed by all three callers — adding a marker means updating all three reader loops (`OKRB` = success but `$IR.RebootRequired`; counted as success everywhere, surfaced as a reboot warning in the manual GUI path via `self._wu_reboot_required`). **All three reader loops go through `wu_core._iter_process_lines`** (reader thread + queue): cancel is checked every 0.5s even when the script emits nothing, and a watchdog taskkills the process after 30 min of silence (`WuProcessAborted('cancel'|'hang')`) — the old direct `for line in process.stdout` blocked forever (dead Cancel button) when the in-script WUA search hung; don't revert to direct iteration. **The "poisoned session" rules (2026-07, field-diagnosed twice on a Dell OptiPlex 7060 — Builds 214 and 218) — all three live in `wu_core` and are used by both AutoFix paths:**
  - `is_reboot_pending(run)` (+`PENDING_REBOOT_PS`): once a *storage/chipset* driver installs (field case: Intel RST `iaAHCIC`), Windows goes pending-reboot and the WUA then returns `orcFailed(4)` for **every remaining package in that session — after a fixed ~143s wait each**. 8 leftovers = ~20 wasted minutes per round. `_install_abort_reason(consecutive_failures, reboot_pending)` is the shared decision: `'reboot'` if pending, else `'failstreak'` after `WU_MAX_CONSECUTIVE_FAILURES` (3) consecutive install failures (the net for a session that rots without setting the flags), else None. It is wired in through the new `abort_check=` parameter of `_iter_process_lines`, which kills the installer process and raises `WuProcessAborted(reason)` — callers never touch the PID.
  - **The pending-reboot query runs only after an install FAILURE, never after `OKRB`** — deliberate: in the field log the OKRB'd Display driver was followed by three more packages that installed fine, so bailing on mere reboot-*need* would sacrifice good installs. Failure is the evidence; reboot-need is not.
  - `verify_failed_installs(failed_titles, pkgs_before, pkgs_after)`: **a `[kód=4]` is not proof of failure.** All 8 "failed" packages in the field log were sitting in the DriverStore afterwards — the WUA verdict was a false negative. After every round the third-party package list is re-read and any failed title whose version shows up among the newly added packages is counted as a **success** (and never added to `install_failed_uids`). Never report a WUA install failure to the user without this check.
  - `pending_reboot_victim(round_failed_titles, aborted_reason)`: **the package that tripped the pending-reboot abort must NOT be blacklisted for the rest of the chain.** `install_failed_uids` (persisted as `wu_failed_uids`) exists so a genuine `[kód=4]` isn't retried at ~2.5 min per leg — but it used to swallow the one failure that is *not* genuine. The abort ordering proves which: `is_reboot_pending` is queried **only after an install failure**, and the first PENDING answer aborts immediately, so the **last** entry of `round_failed_titles` is exactly the package that got its `[kód=4]` from the already-poisoned session; everything before it failed while PENDING was still False and is real. `'failstreak'` gets no mercy (that *is* a real failure run). Field-proven (2026-08-05, Dell Latitude 7400): "Intel Corporation Bluetooth Driver Update (24.40.0.3)" was package 16/20, `[kód=4]` → PENDING → round aborted → permanently excluded, so the chain's remaining **two** legs never retried it and the machine shipped with the 3-months-older 24.20.0.3 — while the cure (a reboot) happened seconds later anyway. The contradiction was already written down one screen higher: the `attempt_counts` comment says *"Lábak közt viszont pont az újraindítás a gyógyír"*, and that reasoning was applied to the per-leg counter but not to the chain-wide blacklist. Cannot loop: on a fresh boot the same package no longer gets a `'reboot'` abort, so a real failure there blacklists normally, and `AUTOFIX_MAX_INSTALL_LEGS` is the backstop. Pure function, offline-tested over every abort reason.
  - `_filter_wu_older_duplicates(matches, wu_by_uid)`: WU offers a package's whole history (field: 10 `iigd_ext` Extension packages for one Intel UHD 630, from 2018), and AutoFix used to install every one of them. Keeps only the newest per `(HardwareID, DriverClass, DriverProvider, DriverModel)` family. That key is deliberately narrow: a wrong merge means a needed driver never installs, while a missed merge only restores the old behaviour.
- `_filter_wu_downgrades` (+`_iso_date_or_none`) is the AutoFix **downgrade guard**: WU can offer a package *older* than the installed driver (field-relevant right after a vendor NVIDIA install); matches whose `DriverVerDate` is strictly older than the installed driver's date are skipped — but NEVER for error-coded devices (any driver beats none), and **NEVER when the currently installed driver is an inbox (Windows built-in) one** (`_is_inbox_driver`: published INF is not `oemNN.inf`, or provider is Microsoft). That second exception is not optional: AutoFix deletes the vendor package, Windows immediately drops its own generic driver on the device, and an inbox driver's date is always newer (it ships with the OS) — so a pure date comparison locks the vendor driver out *forever*. Field-proven (2026-07, X670): `atihdwt6.inf` (AMD HDMI audio, 2021-07-13) was skipped in all three rounds because the inbox `hdaudio.inf` replacing it reports 2026-05-16, and the machine ended the run with one fewer driver than it started with. This is why `_get_installed_driver_info` also selects `DriverProviderName` + `InfName`. The manual scan doesn't filter, it only flags (`downgrade: true` pool items render an orange warning and are not pre-selected) — using the same inbox exception.
- **Printer protection 2.0 (`app/wu_core.py`: `_collect_printer_protection` + `_is_printer_protected`, used by BOTH AutoFix paths)**: class-based skipping (`AUTOFIX_PRINTER_SKIP_CLASSES`) alone was NOT bulletproof — multifunction printer packages spread across USB/Ports/SYSTEM classes (field-seen: `mvusbews.inf`, `hppscnd.inf`, `hpbuio70l.inf`), and deleting those broke customer printers/scanners. Before third-party deletion the code now also protects (a) every INF actually used by present printing components (`Get-PrinterDriver` InfPath + present Printer/PrintQueue/Image devices' `DEVPKEY_Device_DriverInfPath`) and (b) every package whose provider matches a printer vendor present on the machine (`PRINTER_VENDOR_KEYWORDS`; deliberately over-protective — WU restores e.g. HP laptop drivers anyway, a broken customer printer is unrecoverable). **INF matching must check both the published (`oemXX.inf`) and original (`hpc1320u.inf`) names** — `Get-PrinterDriver` reports original names, PnP `DriverInfPath` reports published ones (live-verified on the shop machine). **The same protection is available for MANUAL deletion since 2026-08-28** (explicit user decision: *"ha manualisan akarom csinalni az egeszet akkor nem tudom a nyomtatokat kiszurni egyetlen kapcsoloval"*): the Drivers view has a `hide-printer-drivers` toggle, **default on**, that filters printer packages out of the deletable table — `GuiDriversMixin.get_printer_driver_infs(known_drivers)` calls **the very same two functions**, so the manual list and the fix's protection cannot drift apart (a second, class-only implementation here would hide `mvusbews.inf`-class packages from the fix but not from the technician's Delete button). Three rules: it takes the **already-loaded** driver array from JS rather than re-running `dism /Get-Drivers` (15–50 s — same trick as the AutoFix delete preview); while the detection is in flight `printerInfs` is `null` and **nothing is hidden**, because an empty set would falsely imply the machine has no printer drivers (the toggle shows `⏳ felderítés...` meanwhile); and toggling it **drops the selection of any row it hides**, or a printer package ticked while the filter was off would still be deleted — the exact outcome the switch exists to prevent. Measured live on the dev box: 4 of 6 test packages classified as printer, including the Samsung `mvusbews.inf` in class `USB` that a class-only filter misses, and the vendor keyword set resolved to `canon/epson/hewlett/konica/minolta/samsung` — no generic vendor, so the filter cannot swallow unrelated packages.
- **NIC safety net (`app/wu_core.py`: `_export_net_driver_backup`/`_restore_net_driver_backup`, both AutoFix paths)**: AutoFix deletes the LAN/Wi-Fi driver too, and if neither inbox nor WU covers the NIC (field-seen: fresh AM5 + Realtek 2.5GbE), the machine strands offline while the chain needs internet. Before deletion, Net-class packages are exported (`pnputil /export-driver`) to `<app_data>\netdrv_backup`; on the resume leg (GUI) / after install (CLI), if `_check_internet()` fails the backup is reinstalled + rescan.
- **Wi-Fi-s telepítés — the AutoFix's fourth checkbox (`--wifi-mode`, 2026-08-05, explicit user decision)**: until this, AutoFix was only reliable on a **wired** machine ("wifivel nem megy"), for three independent reasons, all of which had to be fixed together. (1) **`_check_internet` is a single 3 s TCP probe**, and the chain runs it right after logon — on Ethernet the link is up at boot, but a Wi-Fi association + auth + DHCP takes 15–45 s (more on 802.1X), so the chain read "no internet" when it would have had one half a minute later, and dropped into the rescue branch for nothing. The fix is `GuiBaseMixin._wait_for_internet(timeout, task_id, reason)`, which polls every ~2 s and reports progress; `AUTOFIX_NET_WAIT_WIFI` = 120 s, `AUTOFIX_NET_WAIT_WIRED` = 45 s (wired waits too — slow switch/DHCP could hit the same wall). It costs nothing in the normal case: the first probe returns immediately. (2) **The delete phase removed the Wi-Fi driver.** On Ethernet that is usually harmless (Windows' inbox LAN driver takes over); on Wi-Fi many Intel/Realtek/Qualcomm cards have no usable inbox driver, so the machine strands with no connectivity at all. (3) **Even if the driver returns, the saved network can be lost**: WLAN profiles hang off the interface GUID (`Wlansvc\Profiles\Interfaces\{GUID}`), and a driver remove+reinstall can regenerate it, orphaning the profiles. — The design (chosen over delete-and-restore): with the box ticked, the **connected** Wi-Fi adapter's driver is *protected from deletion*, exactly like the printer opt-out — `wu_core.collect_wifi_protection`/`is_wifi_protected`, matching **both** published and original INF name. This does not freeze the driver: the install phase can still put a newer version on top. **The Wi-Fi adapter is looked up whether or not it is CONNECTED** (2026-08-07): `_WIFI_DETECT_PS` used to enumerate only `Status -eq 'Up'` adapters, so on a laptop sitting on the cable — the exact machine where a tech ticks "I'll switch to Wi-Fi during the fix" — no adapter was found, nothing was protected, and **no package could land in the preview's `wifi` group**, i.e. the Wi-Fi checkbox visibly did nothing to the delete list. `detect_wifi_state` now reports `present` (a Wi-Fi card exists) separately from `wifi` (it is associated): `wifi` still decides whether the dialog pre-ticks itself, `present` decides whether there is anything to protect once the tech ticks it by hand. An `Up` adapter always wins the pick, so a disabled second card cannot displace the working one. Since `collect_wifi_protection` is only consulted by the delete phase **in Wi-Fi mode** (the preview always calls it and lets the UI decide), the widening cannot protect anything the user did not ask for. It also logs a WARNING when a Wi-Fi card exists but no third-party package matches it — otherwise the "the switch locks nothing" report is unanswerable.

The adapter is identified with `Get-NetAdapter`/`Get-NetConnectionProfile` (structured objects), **never `netsh wlan show interfaces` text** — that output is localized, and this project already has a scar from parsing localized console text (see the Hungarian pnputil stems in `delete_succeeded`). **Remembering the Wi-Fi password and auto-reconnecting** is part of the deal, and it has three moving parts, all of which were needed: (a) `export_wlan_profiles` saves every WLAN profile *with its key* before deletion; (b) the **active SSID is persisted to `autofix_stats.json` (`wifi_ssid`), not just to the instance** — the legs are separate processes, so the original `self._autofix_wifi_ssid` was always empty by the time `_autofix_recover_wifi` ran on the install leg, and the rescue could not tell which of the saved networks to return to (found 2026-08-05 while wiring this up); (c) the remembered network is forced to `connectionmode=auto` (`wlan_set_autoconnect`) — the chain does 3–4 **unattended** reboots, and a customer profile set to manual connect would bring the machine up with no network every single time, no matter how well we remembered the password. The reconnect itself is two-stage: first a plain `wlan_connect` with the remembered SSID (the common case — profile and key are intact, Windows just did not re-associate), and only if that fails the full profile re-import, which is what covers the orphaned-profile case (new interface GUID after a driver reinstall). Both the post-reboot path and the closing driver-swap path go through the same `_autofix_recover_wifi`. **`key=clear` is deliberately NOT passed** — that would write the customer's Wi-Fi password to disk in plaintext; without it the keyMaterial stays DPAPI-encrypted, which re-imports fine on the same machine+user (the resume legs run as `-UserId $env:USERNAME`, so that holds), hence `netsh wlan add profile … user=current`, not `user=all`. `clear_wlan_backup()` deletes the folder at the end of the chain — no network profile files are left on a customer machine. The checkbox is **auto-detected**: `get_autofix_net_state()` pre-ticks it when the machine is on Wi-Fi *and* has no active wired link (the dialog opens instantly and the tick arrives asynchronously, since the detection is a PowerShell call). Like every other dialog choice it rides the chain as a flag appended by **`_schedule_autofix_resume` itself, never by the call sites** — offline-tested over all 16 checkbox combinations. UI: deliberately a **bigger, highlighted block** above the three small toggles (`mc-wifi-mode`), because it is not fine-tuning — it states what network the machine is on, and the whole chain's survival depends on it. GUI-only (the CLI AutoFix is one-shot with no reboot chain, so it has no legs to survive). **The protected package is still exported to `netdrv_backup`** even though it is never deleted — it is the rollback copy, it costs seconds, and its absence would be an offline customer machine.
- **Wi-Fi mode does NOT freeze the driver, and the old one is swept at the very end (`_replace_old_wifi_driver`, 2026-08-05, user's own design and it is the better one)**: the Wi-Fi opt-out skips **deletion only** — `filter_autofix_risky_devices` gates storage/firmware and nothing else, so the adapter stays in the WU matching *and* the catalog closing round and gets the newest driver like every other device. What the protection costs is therefore not "an up-to-date driver", only the strip-and-reinstall — which matters solely for a *corrupt* driver. The remaining gap is the leftover: after an in-place update the **old** package sits in the DriverStore. `auto_cleanup_duplicates` already removes it when the vendor kept the original INF name (`netwtw10.inf` v22 → v23, same group); it does **not** when the vendor *renamed* (`netwtw08.inf` → `netwtw10.inf`), because those are different groups. So at the very end of the chain — after the duplicate cleanup, before the summary — this step checks whether the adapter now runs on a **different** INF than the protected old one, and if so retires the old package. **The order is the whole point and must not be inverted into "download → delete → install"**: here the new driver is already installed and *proven working*, so the machine is never left without a network driver, whereas pre-staging leaves a window where it runs on neither. Five guards, any one of which keeps the old package (keeping it is never an error): the device must be on a different INF; the internet must work; the old published name must still map to the same original INF (renumbering guard); it must not appear in `get_active_published_infs` (`None` ⇒ do nothing); and the delete is a plain `pnputil /delete-driver` — no `/uninstall`, no `/force`, so anything still using it simply refuses. Connectivity is re-checked **after** the delete, and a loss is reported loudly with the USB→RJ45 remedy — the tech must see it, not the next customer. The closing summary states which of the two outcomes happened. Offline-tested over all ten branches.
- **Wi-Fi driver FULL REBUILD — the fifth checkbox (`--rebuild-wifi-driver`, `mc-rebuild-wifi`, 2026-08-06, explicit user decision)**: the rule above closes the "up-to-date driver" gap but not the *corrupt* one, and the difference is narrower than it sounds — it is exactly **the adapter's driver key** (Device Manager → Advanced: power saving, 802.11 mode, roaming aggressiveness). An in-place upgrade merges the new INF's values into the existing key and leaves everything it does not mention; only `/uninstall` detaches the driver from the *device*, which is what makes Windows rebuild that key from scratch. That matters because a classic field symptom — "the network printer keeps dropping" — is usually the adapter's power-saving setting, not the driver files. So with the box ticked the delete phase, **as its last action before the reboot**, runs `wu_core.rebuild_wifi_driver`: `pnputil /delete-driver <wifi.inf> /uninstall /force` → re-add from a **dedicated** backup folder (`netdrv_backup\wifi`, exported by `export_wifi_driver_backup`) → `/scan-devices` → `restore_wlan_profiles` → `_wait_for_internet(AUTOFIX_NET_WAIT_WIFI)`. Load-bearing details: (a) **the placement is the safety net** — the leg reboots right afterwards, so the existing post-reboot rescue (`_wait_for_internet` → `_autofix_recover_wifi` → `_restore_net_driver_backup`) heals any failure by itself; putting this at the *end* of the chain would leave a reboot-requiring driver (3010) offline with nothing left to run; (b) **the backup folder is separate from `netdrv_backup`'s root** — that root also holds the *wired* NIC's old package, which the chain deleted on purpose so WU can reinstall it fresh, and a `*.inf /subdirs` restore from the root would put it straight back; (c) **nothing is deleted unless there is something to restore** (`wifi_backup_has_inf`) and a failed removal never proceeds to the install, so the machine keeps a working driver in both cases; (d) the step is **skipped entirely when Wi-Fi mode is off** — the adapter's driver is then deleted with all the others in the normal phase, so the rebuild already happens; the UI shows the checkbox greyed-out-but-ticked on a wired machine because that is the truth, not a setting. Default **on** in both cases (explicit user decision: "ha nincs wifin akkor egész nyugodtan cserélheti"), unticking it in Wi-Fi mode falls back to the update-only behaviour with no connection loss at all. The passwords survive because `export_wlan_profiles` already saves **every** profile with its (DPAPI-encrypted, never plaintext) key and `wlan_set_autoconnect` forces `connectionmode=auto`. Offline-tested over all outcomes (success, no backup, failed removal, 3010, empty list) plus all 32 flag combinations of the propagation.
- **NIC rescue pack — REMOVED (explicit user decision, 2026-07-28)**: `app/nicpack_core.py` + `app/gui/nicpack.py`/`app/cli/nicpack.py`, the GUI "🛟 LAN Mentőcsomag" button/chip, the CLI menu item AND the AutoFix no-net last-resort branch are all deleted. It solved the no-NIC-driver → no-internet bootstrap via a hand-built `nicpack.zip`, but the shop never used it — their standing practice is a USB→RJ45 adapter with a built-in driver, which covers the same emergency with zero maintenance (the zip was a manually curated release asset that would have rotted). The AutoFix no-net message now recommends exactly that adapter. The `netdrv_backup` export/restore safety net (which backs up the machine's own Net drivers before deletion) is unrelated and **stays**. Don't reintroduce the pack; if the no-net rescue ever needs strengthening, extend `netdrv_backup`, not a curated zip.

### Vendor-site driver sources (GPU + motherboard)

> **A VIDEOKÁRTYA-GYÁRTÓI ÁG (NVIDIA/AMD/Intel) TELJESEN TÖRÖLVE 2026-09-02** (explicit user decision): *„ez a videokartya cucc nem is kell a programba, a program amugyis felrak egy videokartya drivert igyis ugyis… a manual scannél se legyen videokartyas cucc"*. Törölve `app/gui/nvidia.py` és `app/gui/vendorgpu.py`, a `GuiNvidiaMixin`/`GuiVendorGpuMixin` mindkét API-osztályból, a szken végi három hívás, a CLI két menüpontja (`_nvidia_flow`, a `_vendor_pages` GPU-ágai) és a frontend három kártyája.
>
> **AMIT EZZEL TUDVA ELENGEDTÜNK** (a felhasználó a döntés előtt megkapta): az NVIDIA-ág nem link-out volt, hanem **letöltötte és csendben telepítette** (`-s -noreboot`) a gyári drivert, ami jellemzően hónapokkal újabb a WU-énál (mért: GT 710 → 475.14, RTX 3060 → 610.74). NVIDIA-s gépen mostantól a WU/katalógus verziója marad; a gyári driverhez kézzel kell a GeForce-oldalra menni.
>
> **AZ INTEL-ÁG AMÚGY IS HALOTT VOLT — mérve 2026-09-02**, mielőtt bármit töröltünk: az `intel.com/.../785597` oldal **HTTP 403 Forbidden**-t ad *minden* úton — a kódban lévő `Mozilla/5.0`-val, teljes Chrome UA-val, Accept+nyelv fejlécekkel, és a projekt PowerShell-fallbackjével is (`Access Denied`). Akamai-szintű bot-védelem, nem User-Agent kérdés. Vagyis az Intel-kártya **soha nem jelent meg**, csendben elbukott (fail-silent hibakezelés). Az AMD-ág ezzel szemben működött (`amdversions.xml`, 1,3 mp, 89 KB). **Ha valaki valaha visszahozná a GPU-ágat: az Intelre az oldal-scrape nem járható út.**
>
> **KÉT DOLOG SZÁNDÉKOSAN MEGMARADT**, és mindkettőt a törlés közben kellett megmenteni: (a) `open_vendor_driver_page` átkerült a `vendorgpu.py`-ból az **`oemdrivers.py`**-ba, mert a gép/alaplap-gyártói link-kártya is azt hívja; (b) a frontend `openVendorPage()` függvénye — ez a törléskor a két eltávolított render-függvény KÖZÖTT állt, először velük együtt esett ki, és a hiba úgy nyilvánult volna meg, hogy **az OEM-kártya gombja némán nem csinál semmit**. Az alábbi szekció többi része a *motherboard/OEM link-out* ágra továbbra is érvényes.

- **Vendor-latest NVIDIA driver check (`app/gui/nvidia.py`)**: WU's GPU drivers lag months behind; after each manual scan `_check_nvidia_driver()` queries NVIDIA's public services (lookup XML TypeID=3 cached 7 days in app-data → `AjaxDriverService DriverManualLookup`, DCH first then standard; osID 135 for Win11 build≥22000 else 57) for the *real* PCI NVIDIA GPU (`VEN_10DE` — virtual displays excluded), converts installed `DriverVersion` `32.0.15.6636` → NVIDIA `566.36` (last-5-digits rule), and emits `nvidia_driver_info` for the UI card; `install_nvidia_driver()` downloads with MB progress and runs the installer silently (`-s -noreboot`, returncode 1 = ok-but-reboot; cancel works during download only — never kill a GPU installer mid-flight). Product matching: exact name else longest-substring (so "RTX 3060" doesn't shadow "RTX 3060 Ti"); everything is fail-silent so a scan can never break on NVIDIA's servers. Live-verified: GT 710→475.14, RTX 3060→610.74.
- **AMD/Intel vendor check (`app/gui/vendorgpu.py`) + OEM card (`app/gui/oemdrivers.py`) — LINK-OUT ONLY, deliberately**: unlike NVIDIA, AMD/Intel have no stable public download API, and both block referer-less direct exe downloads — so these cards compare versions and open the official page (`open_vendor_driver_page`, https-only guard), they must NOT be "upgraded" to auto-download scraped installers. AMD's latest comes from the *official* GPUOpen `amd-vulkan-versions` repo `amdversions.xml` (cached 3 days; its `<windows-version>` e.g. `32.0.31021.5001` compares directly against `Win32_VideoController.DriverVersion`; newest WHQL by release-date). Intel's comes from the stable-ID download page 785597 (highest `\d+.0.101.\d+` in the page text) and is offered **only** for the modern unified driver family (installed version matches `^\d+\.0\.101\.` or an Arc card) — legacy iGPUs can't apply it. The OEM card deep-links Dell by Service Tag (`.../servicetag/<tag>/drivers`), Lenovo by serial search, HP generic page + serial shown for manual entry. **When `Win32_ComputerSystem.Manufacturer` is a placeholder (`To Be Filled By O.E.M.`, `Default string`, …) — i.e. a self-built machine, which is a large share of shop intake — it falls back to `Win32_BaseBoard` (`BOARD_VENDOR_PAGES`: ASRock/ASUS/MSI/GIGABYTE/Biostar) and shows the board's support page + the board model to type into their search.** **[NAGYRÉSZT VISSZAVONVA 2026-09-07 — a link-kártya marad, az INDOKLÁSA viszont téves volt]** ~~That fallback exists because the vendor chipset/audio/LAN drivers for desktop boards are only available there: WU does not offer them, so on a self-built box the onboard audio stays on Microsoft's generic `hdaudio.inf` and the NIC on the inbox driver (field-confirmed 2026-07-24 on an ASRock B450M Pro4 — both still generic after a full, error-free AutoFix chain)~~, and before this the card was simply skipped, leaving the tech no pointer at all.

> **AZ „ÖSSZERAKOTT GÉPEN AZ ONBOARD HANG GENERIKUS MARAD" ÁLLÍTÁS MA MÁR NEM IGAZ — a program megtalálja és fel is rakja a gyári drivert** (terepen mérve, 2026-09-04, **ASRock B450M-HDV R4.0**, Build 302; a felhasználó vetette fel, hogy a fájl ezt még mindig állítja). A napló ugyanabból a láncból:
>
> ```
> 21:27:31  [CATALOG] High Definition Audio Device: a gyártó ehhez a géptípushoz
>           (HDAUDIO\FUNC_01&VEN_10EC&DEV_0897&SUBSYS_18493897&REV_1004) 75 csomagot publikál
> 21:28:14  ✅ High Definition Audio Device telepítve!
> 21:28:26  ✅ High Definition Audio Device: gyári driver működik (eddig Microsoft alapdriver volt).
> 21:28:15  ✅ Realtek PCIe GbE Family Controller telepítve!
> ```
>
> Vagyis a **hang ÉS a LAN is** gyári drivert kapott, a záró egészségjelentésben pedig egyetlen eszköz maradt alapdriveren (a monitor). Ez nem véletlen, hanem a 2026-09-01/09-02-i javítások eredménye: az alaplap SAJÁT `&SUBSYS_`-kulcsán mélyítünk (`_catalog_fetch_rows(deep=True)`), és inbox-driveres eszköznél a régebbi kiadás is legitim tartalék. A 2026-07-24-i mérés akkor igaz volt; azóta a kód változott, és **az akkori következtetést a mérés megcáfolta**.
>
> **Amit a kártya viszont továbbra is ér, és amiért marad:** a gyártói oldalon lévő csomag gyakran frissebb a katalógusénál, van hozzá vezérlőpult/segédszoftver, és marad néhány eszköz (BIOS-segédek, RGB/ventilátor-vezérlés), amire tényleg nincs katalógus-csomag. A link tehát *kiegészítés*, nem az egyetlen út.
>
> **NE OLVASD ÚGY, HOGY „MINDIG TALÁL"**: ugyanennek a gépnek egy másik hang-csomópontja (`High Definition Audio Controller`, az AMD busz-vezérlő) **elvesztette** az `oem6.inf`-et (AMD 24.10.0.1), és `hdaudbus.inf`-en maradt — ezt 2026-09-07 óta a záró jelentés külön, nevesítve ki is mondja. A két eset könnyen összekeverhető, mert a nevük hasonló: a **Device** a Realtek codec (megkapta a gyárit), a **Controller** az AMD busz (nem kapta vissza). Board links are deliberately the vendor's **support/download landing page**, not a model deep-link (those rot, and MSI/Gigabyte sit behind bot protection) — same reasoning as the HP branch. Only `_is_placeholder`-filtered values are trusted; an unknown board vendor still emits nothing. The card payload carries `title`/`desc` from Python (`renderOemCard` in ui.html falls back to the old wording) because the OEM copy ("gép", "dokkoló") is wrong for a motherboard. All three run at the end of `start_hw_scan`, fail-silent like the NVIDIA check. (A full OEM *catalog* integration — Dell CatalogPC.cab / Lenovo per-MT XML with silent installs — was consciously deferred: blind-shipping untested silent OEM installers to customer machines is riskier than the link-out.)

### DriverStore duplicate cleanup & boot repair

- **DriverStore duplicate cleanup (`app/gui/dupdrivers.py`; the GUI panel lives in the *Temp Fájlok Törlése* view since 2026-07 — it used to sit in the Drivers view, whose freed spot now holds the AutoFix quick-launch hero card)**: groups third-party packages by *original* INF name, keeps the **newest release** per group (date first, version only as a same-date tiebreak — `dup_release_key`, same rule as everywhere else since 2026-07-27; the date comes from the DISM `Date :` line, which `parse_dism_driver_list` now parses into an ISO `date` field), offers the rest for deletion. Version-only sorting used to mark the *newer* package as the deletable duplicate whenever a vendor renumbered its scheme; when date and version disagree the group logs `[DUPDRV] … a DÁTUM dönt, nem a verzió` with both candidates, since that is the only line answering "why did it keep the lower version number?". Packages with no parseable date fall back to the old version ordering. Safety rules baked in: published INFs actively used by present devices (`Win32_PnPSignedDriver` `InfName` set) are never deletable (shown locked); if that query fails the whole delete is refused (`active_infs is None` → everything marked active); **and an EMPTY set must never be returned — that is a failure, not a result** (2026-08-05, found by the offline test of the deferred INF cleanup): `get_active_published_infs` used to return `None` only on an *exception*, so a PowerShell run that died quietly (empty stdout, or non-JSON output) fell through the `data = []` branch and handed the caller an empty active set — in which **every** package looks unused, silently bypassing the very `None` guard this rule is about. On a live Windows dozens of INFs are always active (measured on the dev box: 63), so zero elements is by definition a failed query. The returncode is deliberately **not** the verdict — the output is: a non-terminating PowerShell error can return 1 with a perfectly good list (the same trap documented for `build_find_bench_tools_ps`), so a non-empty parse is accepted regardless of rc, and an empty/unparseable one is refused regardless of rc. Both consumers (this panel + `auto_cleanup_duplicates`, and now `_finish_deferred_inf_cleanup`) were exposed. Only `oemXX.inf` names are ever deleted; delete tries `pnputil /delete-driver` plain first, `/force` only on failure; **`drivers_core.delete_succeeded` treats `3010` as success on the return code alone** — it is `ERROR_SUCCESS_REBOOT_REQUIRED`, the package *is* gone — and its localized text fallback matches both Hungarian stems (`törl` for törlés/törlése **and** `töröl` for törölve/törölt; one alone misses the other, which silently listed successfully-deleted packages under "nem sikerült eltávolítani"). **The negative markers must be tested BEFORE the positive stems** (`DELETE_FAILURE_MARKERS` then `DELETE_SUCCESS_MARKERS`, 2026-07-26): the text branch only runs when the return code is *not* 0/3010 — i.e. exactly in the failure case — and the Hungarian pnputil failure reads "A(z) oem12.inf illesztőprogram-csomag **törlése** nem sikerült", which contains the positive `törl` stem, so the old positive-only check reported a **failed delete as success**. That is the same silent-false-success class as the Build 218 `0xC0000142` case, and it blanked the AutoFix "could not be removed" list while the phase still printed `✅ Driverek eltávolítva`. The markers are accent-free prefixes where possible (`nem siker`, plus `torl`/`torol`/`eltavolit` mirrors) because pnputil emits ANSI and its output arrives mojibake'd in the field; the delete worker re-checks the active set at delete time (UI state can be stale). **Automatic closing cleanup**: `dupdrivers_core.auto_cleanup_duplicates(run, log, get_drivers)` is the unattended twin (same safety rules, fail-silent — a cleanup error must never fail the install) and runs at the end of every driver install: GUI manual install (`install_selected_wu`, live system only), GUI AutoFix final leg (right before `_emit_autofix_summary`), CLI AutoFix (after FÁZIS 6, only if something installed). **MINDEN TÖRLENDŐ CSOMAGOT NEVÉN KELL NEVEZNI, A MŰVELET ELŐTT** (2026-09-08, Rule 0): a napló 2026-09-08-ig ennyit mondott — `[DUPDRV] 2 duplikátum-csoport, 2 törölhető régi verzió` → `pnputil /delete-driver oem5.inf` → `✅ oem5.inf törölve (1/2)` —, márpedig az `oemNN.inf` szám önmagában semmit nem árul el **és újratelepítéskor átszámozódik**, tehát egy „hova lett a driverem?" bejelentés a naplóból megválaszolhatatlan volt. Az adat végig ott volt a `groups`-ban. Most minden tétel WARNING-gal, az **eredeti INF-névvel, gyártóval, verzióval, dátummal** és a helyette MEGMARADÓ csomaggal megy ki a törlés előtt; a képernyő-szöveg is megnevezi (`duplicate_delete_details` → `delete_duplicate_packages(details=…)`). **A `details` nem kötelező paraméter (a régi hívások változatlanul működnek), de MINDIG add meg** — enélkül a napló újra csak egy `oemNN.inf` számot mutat.
- **BCD button = BootFixer.cmd downloader (`app/gui/bcd.py`)**: per explicit user decision the "BCD Boot Hiba Javítása" GUI button no longer runs the built-in bcdboot/bootrec repair — it downloads the user's own `BootFixer.cmd` (raw.githubusercontent.com/egonixaimgod/boot_javito_tool/main/BootFixer.cmd, same fresh-Windows cert fallback as block.bat) into `_app_data_dir()` and the UI explains how to run it manually; the app NEVER executes it. The built-in automatic repair (after offline driver restore, and the CLI's `repair_bcd_standalone_cli` menu item) lives in `app/bcd_core.py: repair_bcd` — canonical flow: diskpart EFI-partition lookup + `bcdboot /s <EFI> /f UEFI`, falling back to `bcdboot /f ALL`, then `bootrec` — and must never be wired to the GUI button.

### Manual scan pipeline

- **Manual scan — hybrid pipeline (`app/gui/hwscan.py`)**: the scan now (1) surveys installed driver versions+dates once (`_get_installed_driver_info`, returns `{PNP: {'version','date'}}`) and shows "telepítve: X · date" next to every offer (offer side also shows `wu_date` from `DriverVerDate`/catalog row); (2) after a successful WU search runs a **catalog top-up** for error-coded devices WU returned nothing for — the pool is therefore MIXED (WU items carry `update_id`, catalog items carry `url`), so `install_selected_wu` is a **per-item dispatcher**: one `_safe_thread` worker creates a restore point, then calls `_install_wu_api_sync` then `_install_catalog_sync` (both return `(success, fail, cancelled)` and must NOT emit `task_start`/`task_complete` — the dispatcher owns the lifecycle), and on success emits `offer_rescan` (UI banner offering a new scan — chipset/USB drivers surface new devices); (3) emits a `problems` list (devices with `ConfigManagerErrorCode` ≠ 0, code descriptions in `PNP_ERROR_CODE_DESCRIPTIONS`, `has_fix` flag, `pnp_id`) rendered as a red section in the UI so unresolved devices are never silent — rows with quick-fixable codes get a button calling `fix_problem_device(pnp_id, code)` (22 → `Enable-PnpDevice`, others → disable+enable cycle; synchronous, deliberately bypasses `_task_busy`). The old catalog install path downloaded every cab TWICE (a vestigial sequential loop + the threaded worker) — the sequential loop was removed; don't reintroduce it.
- **Manual scan — WU-API timeout policy & Catalog fallback (`app/gui/hwscan.py`)**: `_search_wu_api` runs the WUA search with a 300s timeout; on timeout it restarts the WU services (that heals the *next* search) but deliberately does **NOT** retry the search itself — field-proven (cloned Windows on brand-new AM5 hardware, 2026-07, twice in a row) that the post-restart retry times out identically, doubling the wait to ~10.5 min for zero benefit. Callers handle the `None` return on their own: manual scan falls back to the Catalog, AutoFix breaks out of the WU rounds (and still runs its catalog closing round). The **Catalog engine** (`_catalog_fetch_rows`/`_catalog_find_driver`/`_catalog_search_collect`; `_catalog_search` is the pool-appending wrapper) scrapes `catalog.update.microsoft.com` on 10 threads, and per device queries **all of its *specific* HWIDs** (max 4; `wu_core.is_specific_hwid`, 2026-07-28 — **type-code IDs are never used as a search key**, and a device with no specific ID at all is skipped with a DEBUG line naming the IDs). That rule exists because the Catalog answers a type code with *any vendor's* package for that type, and once round 3 lost its device-level filters the damage was immediate and measured on an Intel Q170 box: `ACPI\PNP0501` (serial port) returned an **LG** package, `ACPI\PNP0A08` and `USB\ROOT_HUB30` each returned an **AMD** package — on an Intel machine. The USB hub is the instructive one: it *has* an Intel-specific ID (`USB\ROOT_HUB30&VID8086&PIDA12F`), but the AMD row pulled in via the generic key had a newer date and won the row selection. Filtering the *query key* rather than the *device* removed all four bogus hits, kept every legitimate one, and halved the round (13.9s → 6.9s). Do not "fix" a wrong-vendor catalog match by excluding the device — check whether a type code got used as a key. The synthesized base ID goes through the same test) plus the synthesized **base vendor HWID** (`wu_core.base_vendor_hwid` — strips `&SUBSYS_`/`&REV_`/`&CC_`), then **unions every returned row into one candidate set** before choosing. Both halves of that are load-bearing and were measured (2026-07-24, Realtek ALC892): (a) the old code stopped at the *first* HWID that returned rows and its comment claimed a more generic HWID "would return the same" — false, the SUBSYS-scoped key returns a 2021 package (6.0.9136.1) while the base key returns the 2026 one (6.0.9992.1); (b) the device's own `HardwareID` list contained **only** SUBSYS-scoped IDs, so without synthesizing the base key that newer package is unreachable entirely. Rows are **scored for the host OS/arch** (`_catalog_row_score` on the tag-stripped `<tr>` text: arm64 rows excluded on x64, Win11-only rows excluded on Win10, Server rows deprioritized; if every row is excluded it falls back to all rows), and among the best-scoring rows the **newest `Last Updated` date wins, with version only as the tiebreak** — *not* highest version: one vendor ships mutually incomparable version schemes for the same device (measured on the Realtek NIC: `Realtek - Net - 1168.19.704.2024` from 2024-07-03 vs `Realtek Net Driver Update (10.79.50.1003)` from 2025-10-02 — by version the 2024 package "wins" by three orders of magnitude). `wu_core._parse_driver_version` (tiebreak only) counts only ≥3-component number sequences as versions, so "2.5GbE" doesn't parse as 2.5; row order on the catalog page is NOT newest-first; it lives in `wu_core` because the AutoFix duplicate/verification filters use it too. Results **that are not a newer *release* than the device's currently installed driver are skipped** (`_get_installed_driver_info`) — without this the fallback re-offered a driver installed 3 minutes earlier, and this same filter is what makes the AutoFix catalog closing round terminate (the WU API path doesn't need it: the server applies `IsInstalled=0`). **"Newer" means newer by DATE, with the version only as a same-date tiebreak** — one shared definition for the whole project, `wu_core.is_newer_release` / `release_rank` (**explicit user decision, 2026-07-27**: "kit érdekel milyen verziót adnak neki, ha dátum szerint van újabb, az mehet fel"). This gate was the last purely version-based decision on the install path (the WU side's `_filter_wu_downgrades` and the catalog's own row selection were already date-first), and the mismatch cost real drivers: measured on the field machine, AMD SMBus sat on **5.12.0.38 from 2017-08-30** while the catalog offered `System Driver Update (2.0.0.26)` from **2025-12-03** — by version the 2017 package "wins" and the box kept a driver eight years old. Do not revert this to a version comparison. Two properties make it safe, both verified against that log: (a) it does not loop, because the catalog's *Last Updated* runs a day **behind** the installed INF's DriverVer date for the same package (AMD PCI `2025-09-08` vs `2025-09-09`, AMD PSP `2026-03-15` vs `2026-03-16`, Realtek NIC `2025-10-02` vs `2025-10-03`), so every already-current package still evaluates to "not newer"; (b) **when the installed driver is an inbox one and the device is not a `generic_ok` candidate, the date rule is skipped and the old version comparison stands** — an inbox driver carries a Windows date (`input.inf` says 2006-06-21), so date-first would silently turn the deep scan into a generic→vendor swapper on exactly the classes (HID, keyboard, mouse) that `mark_generic_replace_candidates` deliberately excludes, and without its rollback verification. That decision stays where it belongs. When date and version disagree the choice is logged at INFO (`[CATALOG] Dátum szerint ÚJABB, verzió szerint nem`), because "why did it install a lower version number?" is otherwise unanswerable from a paste. Install (`_install_catalog_sync(pool, task_id=...)` — task_id lets AutoFix reuse it on the 'autofix' channel) goes cab-download → `expand` → INF-presence check → `pnputil /add-driver /install` (or `dism /Add-Driver` for offline target). **A pnputil "success" is only a real install when something *new* actually landed**: `Added driver packages: 0` (nothing applied) AND the "already exists in the system" case (every `added successfully` line is a re-add of a package already staged in a previous leg — `Added driver packages: N` with N>0 but all marked `(Already exists in the system)`) are both treated as **no-ops** (counted as skipped, never success). The second check is not optional — a generic-replace candidate that stages fine but never *binds* (field: Realtek UAD audio stays on `hdaudio.inf`) would otherwise report "1 installed" every leg forever, an infinite reboot loop (field-seen, Build 228, capped only by `AUTOFIX_MAX_INSTALL_LEGS`). With the no-op detection the candidate installs once, and on the next leg its package is already in the store → 0 installs → the chain terminates on its own. **A pnputil "added successfully" is not proof the device took the driver, either** (2026-07-25, two cases in one run): the Catalog answers a *base* vendor HWID (`HDAUDIO\FUNC_01&VEN_10EC&DEV_0892`) with every OEM-specific variant of that chip, and newest-date wins — which handed an ASRock board a **Clevo-laptop** Realtek package. It staged fine (`Added driver packages: 4`) and never bound; the device stayed on `hdaudio.inf`. Same run, an NVIDIA catalog package (32.0.15.9595) lost to the incumbent 32.0.15.9186 because that one matches a **more specific** HWID (Windows ranks specificity above version/date). Both reported `✅ telepítve`, and the chain re-downloaded them every leg. Two guards now: (a) **before** install, `wu_core.inf_package_applies(ext_dir, dev_hwids)` scans the extracted INFs for a HWID matching the device's *real* `all_hwids` (returns `None` = undecidable → never blocks); (b) **after** install, the device's INF is re-read and compared against `installed_inf` captured at search time — unchanged means it didn't bind, so it counts as **skipped, not success** (crucial: a false success chains another AutoFix leg). Installs that returned `3010`/"reboot is needed" are exempt from (b) — they legitimately bind at next boot (field: AMD PSP). Non-binding items land on `self._catalog_no_bind`, which the AutoFix round persists to `catalog_no_bind` in `autofix_stats.json` so later legs don't re-download them, **and which the closing summary now names to the user** (`_emit_catalog_no_bind`): these are packages the chain proved to exist and be newer, so silently discarding them with the stats file left the shop with no idea a newer driver was available — the remedy (vendor card for a GPU, board vendor's page for onboard devices) is actionable, so it must be printed. **Guard (a) may only veto when we actually parsed the right INF** (2026-07-27, RTX 3060): a driver cab holds several INFs, and `_read_text_best_effort` returned *nothing usable* for the NVIDIA display INF while the ANSI `nvhda.inf` parsed fine — so `inf_ids` was non-empty (158 HDAUDIO ids) and the "no match" branch vetoed a **legitimately newer** package (catalog 32.0.15.9595 > installed 32.0.15.9186), after downloading 1.1 GB for it, on two separate legs.
