Two rules came out of it: `_read_text_best_effort` now detects **BOM-less UTF-16** (the `latin-1` fallback never raises, so such a file decodes "successfully" into `P\x00C\x00I\x00…` and silently yields zero ids), and `inf_package_applies` returns `None` instead of `False` when **not one** extracted id sits on the device's own bus (device is `PCI\…`, ids are all `HDAUDIO\…` ⇒ we did not read the INF that matters). That bus test is what keeps the Clevo-Realtek case working — there the device *and* the INFs are both `HDAUDIO\`, so the veto still fires. Both functions log per-INF id counts, because "which INF did we actually read?" is the only question that could have settled this from the field log.

  **THE CATALOG PAGINATES AT 25 ROWS, AND THE UNSORTED FIRST PAGE IS NOT A STABLE SAMPLE — always request `&scol=DateComputed&sdir=desc`** (2026-08-06, field log + live measurement). `_catalog_fetch_rows` used to issue one unsorted `Search.aspx?q=<hwid>` and decide from whatever 25 rows came back. Measured on the shop's own box: `PCI\VEN_10DE&DEV_2504` reports **"1 - 25 of 546 (page 1 of 22)"** and `HDAUDIO\FUNC_01&VEN_10EC&DEV_0892` **"1 - 25 of 1000"** — i.e. for the GPU we were looking at 4.6% of the packages, with a "Next" link we never followed. Worse, the unsorted page's *composition* is not stable: in the 2026-08-05 run the same audio device, same 3 HWIDs, four rounds, produced **two different winners** (`6.0.9992.1` [2026-05-18] three times, `6.0.10007.1` [2026-06-22] once) — the 5-weeks-newer package simply missed the sample three times out of four. The date-first rule was right; it was just applied to an arbitrary sample. The fix is server-side ordering, not 22 page fetches: **pagination is a plain GET (`&p=<0-based index>`) and sorting is `&scol=<column>&sdir=asc|desc`** — both names come from the catalog's own `SiteConstants.aspx` (`QueryStringPageIndex='p'`, `QueryStringSortColumn='scol'`; sortable columns per the header's `data-columnName`: `Title`, `Products`, `ClassificationComputed`, `DateComputed`, `DriverVerVersion`, `SizeInBytes`). With date-desc, page 1 *is* the newest 25 rows, which is exactly what the decision needs; a further page is fetched only while the newest-date group still fills the page edge (`CATALOG_MAX_PAGES` = 3 = 75 rows). Verified live: three consecutive sorted requests return identical results, and the audio device now deterministically picks the 2026-06-22 package. The ASP.NET `__doPostBack` route is a dead end — posting `__EVENTTARGET=ctl00$catalogBody$nextPageLinkText` with the viewstate lands on `Error.aspx` (measured, three variants); the `p=` parameter is the supported way. A failed sorted request falls back to the unsorted URL (the old behaviour, never worse).

**A RÉGEBBI GYÁRI CSOMAG SOHA NEM JUTOTT EL A TELEPÍTÉSIG — HÁROM, EGYMÁSTÓL FÜGGETLEN KORLÁT MIATT** (2026-09-01, végig mérve, ASRock B450M Pro4 + Realtek ALC892). A 2026-08-26-i szabály („inbox-driveres eszköznél a régebbi kiadás is legitim tartalék") **papíron létezett, a gyakorlatban hatástalan volt**. Terepi panasz: *„nekem nem a legújabb kell hanem az ami működik, lehet az régebbi is!!! a gyári driver mindig jobb mint az alap inbox windowsos generic driver"*. Igaza volt; a három korlát:

1. **A telepítő fixen 3 jelöltet járt végig** (`CATALOG_MAX_CANDIDATES`), miközben a kereső inbox-eszközre gondosan 6-ot állított sorba (`CATALOG_INBOX_FALLBACK_CANDIDATES`) — vagyis pont azokat a tartalékokat vágta le, amikért a szabály született. A találat mostantól `inbox_now` mezőt visz, és a telepítő ez alapján választ korlátot. **Ha egy szabály két helyen érvényesül, mindkettőt meg kell nézni** — ez a fájl legrégebbi visszatérő hibája.
2. **A tartalék-készlet csak a LEGJOBB OS-pontszámú sorokból állt** (`cands = [c for c in scored if c[0] == best_score]`). A pontozó `None`-t ad a valóban KIZÁRT sorokra (arm64 x64-en, Win11-only Win10-en), a 0-3 viszont csak **preferencia**: Win11-es gépen a „windows 11" sor 3 pont, a „windows 10" csak 1. Mérve: a gép saját SUBSYS-kulcsának mind a 41 sora 2019-2021-es, „Windows 10"-es Realtek csomag — tehát a `cands`-ból kiesett, és a tartalékok mind a 2026-os, MÁS gyártóknak szóló sorok lettek (élőben ellenőrizve: 5 tartalék, egyik sem ASRock). Egy Win10-es gyári driver Win11-en is felmegy. Inbox-eszköznél a tartalék-készlet ezért a teljes `scored`, rendezve: **specifikusság → OS-pontszám → dátum**.
3. **A lapozás megállt az 1. lapon.** Alaphelyzetben csak akkor kérünk következő lapot, ha a legfrissebb dátumú csoport átlóg a lap végén — a SUBSYS-kulcs 41 sora viszont 2019-03-04 .. 2021-03-22 között szóródik, tehát a program a 41-ből **25-öt** látott. `_catalog_fetch_rows(deep=True)` elhagyja a holtverseny-feltételt, de **CSAK** Windows-alapdriveres eszköz SAJÁT, `SUBSYS_`-es kulcsán: ott minden sor ehhez a géphez való. Az általános kulcsra sosem mélyítünk (mérve: **1000 sor / 40 lap**, mind más gyártó OEM-változata — ott a mélyítés puszta kéréspazarlás). A gyakorlati költség csekély: egy SUBSYS-kulcs jellemzően 25 sor alatt marad, akkor pedig nincs 2. lap.

**ÉS A HOSSZABB JELÖLT-LISTÁNAK ÁRA IS VAN — `CATALOG_FALLBACK_MAX_BYTES` = 2 GB.** A 6-os korlát a hangkártyán ártalmatlan (a helyes csomag **11,2 MB**), egy Windows-alapdriveren ragadt **videokártya** viszont ugyanezen az ágon megy, és ott egy csomag **1,2 GB** — 6 jelölt akár 7 GB és órák lennének, ami az [elfogadási feltételt](#the-acceptance-criterion-for-the-one-click-fix-press-it-walk-away-come-back-to-a-finished-machine) (20-60 perc, kávészünet) borítaná fel. A korlát a MÁR ELKÖLTÖTT bájtokra néz a következő jelölt indítása ELŐTT, tehát az elsőt mindig végigvisszük. Számokban: 11 MB-os csomagnál mind a 6 jelölt lefut (0,07 GB), 250 MB-osnál is mind a 6 (1,46 GB), 1,2 GB-osnál a második után megáll (2,34 GB). A leállás nem néma - a technikus látja, hogy letöltési korlát miatt maradt ki a többi jelölt.

**A záró bizonyíték** (letöltve + `inf_package_applies`-szal ellenőrizve, telepítés nélkül): a `Realtek - MEDIA - 6.0.9136.1 [2021-03-22]` csomag **11,2 MB**, 135 INF, és `inf_package_applies = True` erre a hangkártyára — vagyis a telepítő most felrakja, a kártya pedig lekerül a generikus `hdaudio.inf`-ről. Ugyanebben a futásban az `ISA bridge` SUBSYS-kulcsa **0 sort** ad: arra tényleg nincs csomag a katalógusban, és ezt a program most ki is mondja (lásd a „Nincs rá való csomag" szekciót). **A gyorsítótár kulcsa ezért tartalmazza a lekérés mélységét is** (`hwid|max_pages|deep`): egy sekélyebb lekérdezés eredményét tilos bőségesként visszaadni egy mélyebb kérésre.

**A TARTÓS NO-BIND TÁR KULCSA A HARDVER-AZONOSÍTÓ TÖRZSE, NEM A PÉLDÁNY-AZONOSÍTÓ — a program a saját emlékezetét törölte** (`_device_stem`, 2026-08-31, mérve a fejlesztői gépen). A `catalog_no_bind.json` a TELJES példány-azonosítót tárolta, annak a farkában viszont ott a PnP **példányszám**, amit a Windows LÉPTET, ha egy eszköz-csomópontot eltávolítanak és újra felderítenek — vagyis pontosan akkor, amikor ez a program `pnputil /remove-device`-t futtat (újrakötés-kör, regresszió-javítás). A gépen a 4 feljegyzésből **2 vált halottá** egyetlen karakteren:

    USB\VID_041E&PID_3274&MI_03\7&887C350&**0**&0003  ->  ...&**1**&0003
    HDAUDIO\...&REV_1003\5&337FEF56&**0**&0001        ->  ...&**1**&0001

  Következmény: a felület a bizonyítottan **nem alkalmazható** csomagot újra ELŐRE BEJELÖLVE ajánlotta (a technikus jelentette: „miért nincs kipipálva ez, meg amaz igen?"), az AutoFix pedig újra letöltötte. A kulcs most a törzs (az utolsó `\` előtti rész) = maga a hardver-azonosító, ami újrafelderítés után is ugyanaz; **mind a 11 párosítási hely ugyanazt a `_device_stem` függvényt hívja** (a tár írása, a törlése bind-siker után, a jelölés, a jelöltszűrés és a már-próbált URL-ek). Két azonos eszköz közös törzsre kerül — ez helyes: ami az egyikre nem alkalmazható, az az ikertestvérére sem. Régi bejegyzésekhez is működik, séma-váltás nélkül. **Tanulság általánosan: a példány-azonosító NEM tartós azonosító ebben a programban, mert mi magunk változtatjuk meg.**

  **A CATALOG TIE IS DECIDED FROM THE DETAIL PAGE'S `Driver Model` FIELD, BEFORE DOWNLOADING** (2026-08-06). `ScopedViewInline.aspx?updateid=<guid>` is a few KB and carries `Driver Model: NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090,…,NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3060,…` — the only pre-download signal of which tied row is meant for this machine. `wu_core.driver_model_rank` scores it 2 (an entry equals the device name) / 1 (containment, or ≥3 shared tokens) / 0, and is used **only to reorder, never to veto** (the field's fuzzy naming — catalog `Realtek High Definition Audio` vs Windows `High Definition Audio Device` — would make a veto lose real drivers). Two guards the offline test forced: the probe **stops at the first exact match** (a tie can be 45 rows — measured on the Realtek NIC — and a fixed probe window could exclude the right row; the test caught exactly that with the correct row at index 7), and the token rule requires a **digit-bearing token in common** when either name has one, otherwise `RTX 4070` "matches" a list of RTX 30-series cards on the shared words `nvidia geforce rtx`. Probing runs *after* the release gate, so it only costs requests on devices we were going to download for anyway.

  **A ROW FOUND VIA THE DEVICE'S OWN SUBSYS KEY BEATS A NEWER ROW FROM THE GENERIC KEY — and on an inbox-driver device an OLDER release is a legitimate fallback** (`spec_by_guid`, `CATALOG_INBOX_FALLBACK_CANDIDATES`, 2026-08-26, field-proven + measured live). `_catalog_find_driver` queries up to 4 HWIDs and **unions every row into one pool**, after which date alone picks the winner. Measured on an ASRock B450M with a Realtek ALC897 (`HDAUDIO\FUNC_01&VEN_10EC&DEV_0897&SUBSYS_18494897`, sitting on inbox `hdaudio.inf`):
  - the device's **own SUBSYS key** returns `Realtek - MEDIA - 6.0.9360.1`, and that package's INF lists **`…DEV_0897&SUBSYS_18494897` verbatim** (145 ASRock ids in it) — it is exactly the right driver;
  - the **generic key** (`…DEV_0897`) returns a *newer* `Realtek … (6.0.10007.1)`, whose INFs are `HDXACERAIO.inf`, `HDXACPAcer.inf`, … — an **Acer** build whose 332 `DEV_0897` entries are all `SUBSYS_1025xxxx` (1025 = Acer) with **zero** generic and **zero** ASRock ids.
  Date-first therefore crowned the Acer package, `inf_package_applies` correctly vetoed it, and the tie-only fallback rule ("a fallback can never be an older release") meant the correct package **was never reachable** — the machine kept Microsoft's generic audio driver while the right driver sat at position 1 of its own search key. Two changes, and note what each is *not*: the winner is still chosen date-first (that stays an explicit user decision), and a device already on a **vendor** driver keeps the old same-date-only behaviour so it can never be downgraded. What changed is only the **fallback list on an inbox-driver device**: it now also admits older releases, ordered **most-specific HWID first, then newest date** (`spec_by_guid` records which of the 4 keys produced each row). Explicit user decision: *"a semminél, az inbox drivernél jobb a régi driver milliószor… ha semmit se tud feltelepíteni, akkor azt ami jó hozzá, de verzióban régebbi, azt rakja fel — és ez ne csak a hangra legyen igaz, MINDENBŐL mindennél legyen ez a szabály"*. Cost in practice is nil: offline-tested against the real rows, the correct package becomes the **first** fallback, and the installer already dedupes by resolved URL and skips packages the persistent no-bind store proved wrong. The measurement that settles the "should we just page deeper?" question: 12 pages / 300 rows of the generic key are **all** published by "Realtek Semiconductor Corp." yet OEM-specific inside (**PONTOSÍTVA 2026-09-02, újramérve — a korábbi "az ASRock egyáltalán nem publikál a Windows Update-re" megfogalmazás FÉLREVEZETŐ VOLT**: a katalógus az ASRock-specifikus `…&SUBSYS_18496893` kulcsra **41 csomagot ad**, 2017-05-26 és 2021-03-22 között [évenként 2017:7, 2018:4, 2019:12, 2020:16, 2021:2], a legfrissebb a `Realtek - MEDIA - 6.0.9136.1`, amit a lánc meg is talál és fel is rak. Ami igaz: a **kiadó** mindegyiknél a Realtek, nem az ASRock — ASRock nevű publisher tényleg nincs, de ASRock-ra indexelt csomag bőven van. Aki ezt "nincs hozzá driver"-ként olvassa, téved.) — so depth was never the problem, ranking was. **A mélyítés haszontalansága 2026-09-02-én újra lemérve, és megerősítve:** a SUBSYS-kulcs 3 lapon (deep) **41 sort** ad, 12 lapon szintén **41-et** — vagyis ott nincs "hátul maradt" csomag, a program mindet látja. Az ÁLTALÁNOS kulcson a mélyítés valóban hoz többet (3 lap = 75 sor [2025-12-01..2026-07-13] → 12 lap = 300 sor [2024-12-26..2026-07-13], 20,6 vs 10,8 mp), de azok a sorok más gyártók OEM-változatai, tehát a nyereség ott nulla. Ezért marad a `CATALOG_MAX_PAGES` = 3 az általános kulcson, és a mélyítés kizárólag az inbox-driveres eszköz SAJÁT SUBSYS-kulcsán.

  **A „MAX 4 HWID" KORLÁT SOHA NEM VÁG — STRUKTURÁLIS OK MIATT, NEM VÉLETLENÜL** (mérve 2026-09-02, HP EliteDesk 800 G2, Intel Q170; a `hwids[:4]` vágás a `_catalog_find_driver`-ben). A kérdés az volt, nyerne-e valamit a 4 → 6 emelés. Nem: a gép **127 PnP-eszközéből NULLÁNAK van 4-nél több specifikus azonosítója**, és ez buszonként rögzített, nem gépfüggő — a Windows PCI-enumerátora **pontosan 4** hardver-azonosítót ad (`VEN&DEV&SUBSYS&REV`, `VEN&DEV&SUBSYS`, `VEN&DEV&CC_ccsspp`, `VEN&DEV&CC_ccss`), a többi busz kevesebbet:

  | busz | eszköz | max specifikus azonosító |
  |---|---|---|
  | PCI | 15 | **4** (mind a 15-nek pontosan 4) |
  | HID | 6 | 3 |
  | USB | 7 | 2 |
  | HDAUDIO | 2 | 2 |
  | ACPI | 33 | 2 |
  | ROOT / SWD / PRINTENUM / STORAGE | 28+ | 0 |

  Vagyis a korlát emelése definíció szerint 0 új lekérdezést adna. **Amit viszont a mérés kihozott, és ami valódi kérdés volt:** a kód a 4. azonosítót lecseréli a szintetizált törzsre (`hwids[:3] + [base]`), tehát PCI-eszköznél a `&CC_ccss` kulcs sosem fut le. Lemérve mind a 14 érintett eszközre, ez **40 új katalógus-sort** hozna 97 mp-ért — de a **nyertes csomag egyetlen eszköznél sem lenne jobb**: az `Intel AMT SOL (COM4)`-nál a 37 új sor mind ugyanannak a `Intel Ports Driver Update (2543.9.0.0)` csomagnak a duplikátuma (a katalógus egy csomagot több GUID alatt publikál — ugyanaz a jelenség, mint az NVIDIA 25 bejegyzésénél), az `Intel SMBus`-nál pedig a nyertes `AlpsAlpine - System [2019-03-04]`-ről `Synaptics - System [2019-08-25]`-re váltana, **azaz az egyik idegen gyártó OEM-bundle-járól a másikra** — mindkettőt az `inf_package_applies` veti el, letöltés után. A `&CC_` kulcsok ugyanis pont az idegen-gyártós OEM-bundle-ök mágnesei: azok az INF-jükben generikus Intel osztálykód-azonosítót deklarálnak. **Tanulság: a `&CC_ccss` kulcs elhagyása a törzs javára HELYES, ne „javítsd meg".** (Mellékesen: ez a mérés mutatta meg, hogy az Intel SMBus nyertese MOST IS egy AlpsAlpine-csomag — az INF-vizsgálat elveti, a tartós no-bind tár megjegyzi, tehát kezelve van, de a letöltés ára egyszer megvan.)

  **AN INF VETO NOW FALLS BACK TO THE NEXT TIED CANDIDATE — but candidates are deduped by DOWNLOAD URL, which is what makes it safe** (2026-08-06). `_catalog_find_driver` returns `alt_candidates` (same score, **same date**, so a fallback can never be an older release than the winner), and `_install_catalog_sync` walks them when `inf_package_applies` returns False, up to `CATALOG_MAX_CANDIDATES` = 3. The dedupe is not an optimization, it is the thing that stops this fix from being worse than the bug: measured live, **the top five tied rows for `PCI\VEN_10DE&DEV_2504` all resolve to the identical cab** (same filename, same 1165.1 MB, same 4-INF list) — the catalog publishes one package as several per-OS-branch entries, so a naive fallback would have downloaded the same 1.2 GB three times. The URL is known only after the (few-KB) `DownloadDialog` call, so the skip is free. A **download** failure never advances the candidate (the network is at fault, not the package). Every vetoed candidate is recorded with its GUID, so a later run skips it without downloading at all.

  **How the 2026-08-05 NVIDIA case actually resolves** — worth knowing before someone "fixes" the veto again. The chain downloaded 1.2 GB, `inf_package_applies` rejected the package, and it was reported as no-bind. Verified afterwards with a 2 MB HTTP range read of the cab header (the CFFILE table sits at the start of a cab, so the file list is readable without the payload): the package contains exactly **`nvhda.inf`, `nvle.inf` (346 KB — the display INF), `nvpcf.inf`, `nvppc.inf`** — so the extraction was complete and the veto was not the truncated-download case. Our parser was independently cleared: run against the installed `nv_dispig.inf` it reads **208 ids, 207 of them PCI, and it does match `PCI\VEN_10DE&DEV_2504`** (the desktop INF lists the bare id), and it reproduces the field log's counts exactly for the companion INFs (`nvhda`=104, `nvpcf`=0, `nvppc`=4). `_INF_HWID_RE` is `re.IGNORECASE`, so casing is not a factor either. What is *not* settled: the catalog's own metadata claims RTX 3060 support for that package while `nvle.inf` yields 50 ids that do not match this card — reading `nvle.inf` itself would need the full 1.2 GB (it sits at uncompressed offset ~1.34 GB in a single-folder cab). Practical impact is nil either way: the vendor card offers 610.88 against the catalog's 595.95. **Do not "solve" this by weakening `inf_package_applies`** — the Clevo-Realtek case is what it exists for.

**Catalog cab downloads get 3 attempts, not one** (2026-07-27): these payloads are hundreds of MB (a GPU package is 1.1 GB), so a mid-transfer `[WinError 10054]` ("connection forcibly closed by the remote host") is ordinary, not exceptional — in the field one killed the NVIDIA download after 18s and it was booked as a permanent failure, while the identical URL came down fine on the next leg. Retries pause 3s and **delete the partial file first** (same reasoning as the stresstools.zip rule: the leftover eats exactly the disk the retry needs), and each attempt logs its number and the final byte count. **The attempt loop covers download + size check + expand + INF-presence, not just the download** (2026-07-28, field log from the dev box): a server can close the connection early *without* an exception — `http.client` returns a short read silently on chunked reads — so the NVIDIA cab arrived as 139 MB of 1,221,644,925 bytes and was logged as `Letöltve … 1. próbálkozásra`; `expand` then failed with rc=1, **which nothing checked**, and the failure surfaced as the misleading permanent error "nincs .inf a csomagban" while the retry designed for exactly this never fired. Hence: (a) the received size is compared to `Content-Length` and a short file is a failed attempt; (b) an `expand` rc≠0 or an INF-less tree is also a failed attempt (corrupt cab → delete cab + ext dir, re-download), not a terminal error. The catalog `pnputil /add-driver` call passes `ok_codes=(0, 259, 3010)` — 259 (no-op/up-to-date) and 3010 (reboot required) are expected outcomes the code already branches on, and they produced 4 false WARNINGs in an otherwise clean field run. `.msu` links go through `wusa /quiet /norestart` (offline: `dism /Add-Package`), `.exe` links are skipped with a clear message (never silently run an unknown installer). **Multi-INF packages: the unused INFs are unstaged right after install** (`_cleanup_unused_staged_infs`, 2026-07-28, field-seen on the dev box): `pnputil /add-driver <dir>\*.inf /subdirs /install` stages EVERY INF in the package whether or not any present device matches it, and one Razer catalog cab carries the whole Razer product line as separate INFs — a single AutoFix run inflated the machine from 19 third-party packages to **219** (measured: 201 staged `rz*` INFs, none bound to any device). The pnputil output tells each INF's fate per block: an INF with an `installed on device` / `up-to-date on device` line is in use by a present device and **stays**; the rest are deleted with a plain `pnputil /delete-driver` (no `/uninstall`, no `/force` — if anything does use one, pnputil refuses and it stays, which the logic tolerates). Runs only for multi-INF packages (a single-INF no-bind is the bind-check's job). **On a reboot-required install (3010) the verdict is DEFERRED, not skipped** (2026-08-05 — the original code just did nothing there, and that hole cost a machine 120 packages): the binding really does settle at next boot, so judging now would be wrong — but *dropping* the package meant its unused INFs stayed in the DriverStore **forever**, since nothing ever came back to them. Field-proven (Dell Latitude 7400): the `Intel(R) PCI Express Root Port #13 - 9DB4` catalog cab carries Intel's **entire** chipset INF collection (ApolloLake, Avoton, Baytrail, Braswell, Broadwell, ColetoCreek, CougarPoint, Crystalwell, Denverton, FPGA, Haswell, IceLake, IvyBridge, IvyTown, JakeTown, KabyLake…), returned 3010, and the machine went from **23 third-party packages to 163** — 143 even after the closing duplicate cleanup, which cannot touch them (they are not duplicates: every one has its own original INF name). Side effect worth remembering: `dism /Get-Drivers` went from 0.6 s to **77 s**. So `defer=True` now records every published INF of that package into **`pending_inf_cleanup.json`** (`_app_data_dir()`, deliberately *not* `autofix_stats.json` — that dies with the chain), and `_finish_deferred_inf_cleanup()` settles it **after a real reboot**: at the start of the next install leg, and — for a deferral in the *last* leg — from a background thread at the next app start (guarded by a file-existence check, so a normal start costs nothing; skipped on resume legs so two pnputil delete runs can't collide). It re-decides from **live system state**, never from the stale pnputil output: the active published-INF set is the judge, and the same-`original`-INF-name check from `_finish_pending_deletes` prevents a renumbered `oemNN.inf` from taking out a fresh package. **The deferral is not paranoia — it is load-bearing**: in that same field log `oem106.inf` (`iaLPSS2_GPIO2_CNL`) showed no `installed on device` line at install time and *did* bind after the reboot, so an immediate cleanup would have deleted a driver the machine uses. All eight branches offline-tested against the log's verbatim pnputil output. pnputil still enforces signature + INF applicability, but catalog matching is inherently less precise than WUA's server-side evaluation: it's the B-plan for machines where the WU Agent is broken, not full parity.
- **Persistent no-bind memory (`catalog_no_bind.json` in `_app_data_dir()`, 2026-07-28)**: the chain-scoped `catalog_no_bind` list in `autofix_stats.json` dies with the chain, so the manual scan run right after a finished AutoFix re-offered — **pre-selected** — the very packages the chain had just proven don't bind (field log, 2026-07-28: 4 of the 5 manual-scan catalog hits were exactly the chain's no-bind list: Clevo-Realtek audio, Razer USB composite, AMD ISA bridge, NVIDIA 32.0.15.9595 — and the user reasonably read that as "AutoFix left drivers uninstalled"). `_install_catalog_sync` now records every no-bind/veto item (key: `(pnp_id, wu_title)`, with reason + date) into the persistent file via `_no_bind_record`, and `_catalog_search_collect` marks matching hits `prev_no_bind` — the UI shows them **unticked** with a `↷ korábban nem vette át az eszköz` badge. Three deliberate limits: it only *marks* (the user can still tick and retry — a wrongly recorded package must stay reachable); the AutoFix rounds do **not** consult it (a fresh chain deletes+reinstalls drivers, so re-trying there is correct, and its own stats list already prevents within-chain repeats); and a later install that the bind-check **verifies** as taken removes the entry (`bound_ok` → `_no_bind_record` removal), so the file can't go stale-forever. A *newer* catalog release has a different title, so it is never masked by an old entry. **Since 2026-08-06 the record also carries the catalog `guid` and the release `date`, and matching is release-aware**: an entry masks (a) the same GUID, (b) the same title, or (c) **an older-or-equal release of the same package family** (`wu_core.catalog_title_family` strips the trailing `(version)`), compared with `release_rank`. Case (c) is the 2026-08-05 field case: the closing round offered the Realtek audio as `6.0.9992.1` [2026-05-18] while the recorded failure was `6.0.10007.1` [2026-06-22] — a different title, so the old key missed it and the chain re-downloaded the same Clevo package. A genuinely **newer** release is still never masked (the vendor may have fixed it). The GUID is also what lets `_catalog_find_driver` drop a known-bad candidate *before* resolving its URL. **The strongest key, however, is the download URL, and it is the one that actually stops the repeat**: measured 2026-08-06, the catalog lists one NVIDIA cab under **25 separate entries** (25 GUIDs, 25 titles), so a GUID/title ban only pushes the next run onto a different entry pointing at the same 1.2 GB file. The record therefore stores `url` too, and `_install_catalog_sync` seeds its per-device `tried_urls` from the store — so a package already proven inapplicable for that device costs **zero** bytes on every later run (the few-KB `DownloadDialog` resolve is all that happens). URLs carry a content hash, so a genuinely newer release gets a different URL and is still tried. **The skip must live in `_install_catalog_sync`, NOT in `_catalog_find_driver`** (Build 264 field run, 2026-08-06): the same chain downloaded the Clevo Realtek package twice — `6.0.10007.1` [2026-06-22] in the catalog round and `6.0.9992.1` [2026-05-18] in the closing round — because they are different titles *and* different URLs, so neither key matched. The family rule (`catalog_title_family` + `release_rank`) closes that, but only at install time: putting it in the search would make the hit **vanish from the manual scan list**, and the standing rule is that we only ever *mark* a known-bad package (unticked + badge), never hide it. One more restriction: the family skip applies **only when the recorded reason was the INF veto** (`nem alkalmazható`) — that is a fact about the INF that a fresh chain cannot change; a recorded *bind* failure can change, so those are always retried. The search side keeps only the exact-GUID filter, and even that never empties the candidate list.

  **A log line on a hot path is measured, not estimated** (same run): the new `[CATALOG] Lekérdezés:` line produced **448 lines, of which 412 were `-> 0 sor`** — 92% noise in a 5 MB rotating file, which is exactly what the Rule-0 counter-rule forbids. It now logs only when the query returned rows (DEBUG) or when something unusual happened — paging or a lost sort (INFO). A zero-row query is not lost information: `install_call_logging` already records every call with its HWID, and the per-device `Döntés` line summarizes the outcome.
- **[VISSZAVONVA 2026-09-03 — A KÉZI SZKEN EZT MÁR NEM ÍRJA KI, CSAK NAPLÓZZA]** (explicit user decision, ugyanattól a felhasználótól: *„ez hogy mennyi eszköz fut a beépített driveren ez se kell ki lehet onnan törölni a faszba"*). A `renderHwInbox` és az `inbox` payload-mező **törölve**; a Python továbbra is kiszámolja és **naplózza**, és az **1 kattintásos fix záró jelentése változatlanul kiírja a képernyőre**. Miért fordult meg: a nézet időközben öt párhuzamos dobozzá hízott, és ez volt az egyik, ami a valódi teendőt (a találati listát) kiszorította — a részletes indoklás és a helyette bevezetett szerkezet a [nézet-átépítés szekciójában](#a-driver-keresés-nézet-átépítése--öt-dobozból-egy-lista-2026-09-03). Az alábbi eredeti bejegyzés a **szűrő-megosztás** szabálya miatt marad itt: az továbbra is érvényes, mert a záró jelentés ugyanezt a `_health_report_worth_listing`-et hívja. **UGYANEZ A SZABÁLY EGY HARMADIK KÉPERNYŐRE IS KITERJEDT (2026-09-08):** a katalógus-kör *„⚠️ N eszközhöz a katalógusban NINCS megfelelő csomag"* figyelmeztetése ugyanezekre a feltételekre épül (`wu_core.no_source_is_actionable`) — mert terepen egy `machine.inf`-en futó eszközre a katalógus-kör teendőt írt ki, a záró jelentés viszont (helyesen) meg sem említette, azaz **ugyanaz a futás két különböző dolgot állított ugyanarról a gépről**. Ha bármelyik szűrő változik, mindhárom helyet nézd meg. ~~**A KÉZI SZKEN IS KIÍRJA, MI MARADT A WINDOWS BEÉPÍTETT DRIVERÉN**~~ (`inbox` a `hw_scan_result`-ban, `renderHwInbox`, 2026-08-31, explicit user decision: *„írja már ki a program hogy hány driver fut a windows beépített generikus driverén… hogy tudjam mik nem kapnak drivereket"*). Ez a szken **legcsendesebb kimenetele**: az eszköz hibakód nélkül működik, csak épp a Microsoft általános driverén — tehát a találati listán SEHOL nem jelenik meg, mert nincs rá találat. Eddig ezt csak az AutoFix záró jelentése mondta ki, a kézi szken után a technikusnak nem volt honnan tudnia. **UGYANAZT a szűrőt hívja, amit a záró jelentés** (`_health_report_worth_listing`) — két külön lista előbb-utóbb ellentmondana egymásnak ugyanarról a gépről. A szűrő szándékosan szűkebb, mint a keresésé (a katalógust mindenre megkérdezzük, de csak azt írjuk ki, amivel a szerelőnek TEENDŐJE lehet); a többit egyetlen szám összegzi, a *„ehhez gyári driver nem is létezik"* magyarázattal. Amire van találat a listán, az zöld jelet kap: ott nincs teendő, elég feltelepíteni a szken eredményét.
- **"Vendor driver instead of the Windows generic one" (`wu_core.mark_generic_replace_candidates` + the `generic_replace` flag; used by BOTH the manual scan and AutoFix)**: the WU Agent never offers a replacement for a device Windows considers fine, so a machine can finish a flawless AutoFix chain with its onboard audio on Microsoft's generic `hdaudio.inf` and its NIC on the inbox driver — while the chip vendor's own package sits in the Microsoft Update Catalog (measured 2026-07-24, ASRock B450M Pro4: Realtek MEDIA 6.0.9992.1 and Realtek Net 10.79.50.1003 both present in the Catalog, zero offers from the WU Agent). Devices whose current driver is inbox (`_is_inbox_driver`) are therefore marked `generic_ok` and fed to the Catalog engine alongside the error-coded ones — the manual scan lists them with a `🏭 most Windows alapdriver → gyári elérhető` badge (pre-selected, the tech can uncheck), the AutoFix closing round installs them automatically. Rules that must not be softened:
  - **For a marked device the version comparison is SKIPPED entirely.** An inbox driver's version is the Windows build (`10.0.26100.8457`) and the vendor's is its own scheme (`6.0.9992.1`), so the generic always looks "newer" and the better driver would always lose. Same trap the WU path already handles in `_filter_wu_downgrades`. This cannot loop: after a successful install the device runs on `oemNN.inf` with a vendor provider, so `is_generic_replace_candidate` no longer marks it (and `added_zero` already guards the AutoFix reboot loop).
  - **Candidate selection has NO class filter** (2026-07-28, explicit user decision — "ITT IS MINDENT KERESSEN, kivéve amit checkboxszal nem engedek"). It used to carry both a 16-entry class whitelist (`GENERIC_REPLACE_CLASSES`) and a class blocklist (`GENERIC_REPLACE_BLOCKED_CLASSES`); **both constants are gone**. What killed them was an asymmetry nobody could defend: a printer / keyboard / USB controller sitting on a Windows inbox driver was never asked about here, while the *same* device on a vendor driver was picked up fine by the deep scan — i.e. a device got no better driver *because* it was on a worse one. What remains is **only** the two checkbox-gated risk classes, exactly as in the WU path and the deep scan (`is_generic_replace_candidate(dev, inst, allow_storage=, allow_firmware=)`; manual scan passes True/True with the red badge, AutoFix passes the dialog's values). **The bus-enumerator INF blocklist and the vendor-coded-HWID requirement were removed from this round too** (same day, same decision: "menjen ki minden driver, mindenre keressen, maximum nem talál"). Measured live on an Intel Q170 box: candidates went 6 → 66, the round took **6.9s on 10 threads**, and it found **two genuinely new packages the old filters had been hiding** — `Intel - System 11.7.0.1019` on a `machine.inf` "Motherboard resources" node and `Intel - System 9.21` on the HD Audio *controller*. So the old belief that these devices "have no vendor driver at all" was simply wrong for chipset nodes. If this round ever gets slow, the fix is more threads or a result cache — **not** a new device filter. **Consequence to keep in mind: with the storage checkbox ON, this branch can swap the inbox NVMe/AHCI driver for a vendor one, and `_verify_generic_replacements` cannot catch that failure** — it surfaces at the next boot. That is the informed trade the checkbox exists to offer (its warning text names recovery media); it is not a reason to reintroduce a class ban. **Processor is a measured non-candidate, not a banned one** (2026-07-28, live query): a CPU enumerates as `ACPI\GenuineIntel_-_Intel64_Family_6_Model_94` and the Catalog returns **0 rows** for it (and for `ACPI\ACPI000C`), while the PCI chipset device on the same machine returned 25 — so the AMD/Intel "chipset driver" reaches the box through its PCI devices (rounds 1 and 4), never through the processor node. Relaxing `_VENDOR_HWID_RE` to admit `ACPI\` would buy network round-trips and nothing else. **Display is NOT excluded** (and the code comment claiming it was is gone as of 2026-07-28): there is no class list left to exclude it from — this branch only ever runs on a device *currently on an inbox driver*, i.e. a machine stuck on the Microsoft Basic Display Adapter, where the "the vendor card is a better source" argument has nothing to work with (AMD/Intel are link-out only and the NVIDIA check runs in the manual scan alone). On a machine already on a vendor GPU driver the device is not a candidate at all, so the vendor cards keep their monopoly exactly where it matters.
  - **Every replacement is verified and rolled back if it fails** (`_verify_generic_replacements`, runs after the batch): the published `oemNN.inf` names are captured from the pnputil output, the devices' `ConfigManagerErrorCode` is re-read once the PnP stack settles, and any device that went error-coded gets its freshly installed package deleted + a rescan, at which point Windows re-binds the inbox driver automatically. **Error code 0 alone is not success** — it also re-reads the device's INF, because a package can install cleanly while the device stays on the Microsoft driver (field 2026-07-25: the run printed `✅ gyári driver működik` for the Realtek audio and, minutes later in the same summary, correctly listed that device as still on `hdaudio.inf`). Still-inbox after install is reported as a warning, not a success, and nothing is rolled back (the generic driver never left). A device that vanished from the present-device list is reported but **not** rolled back (typically an unplugged USB device — don't discard a good driver over a query miss). This "try it, verify, revert" net is only sound because the inbox driver never leaves the machine, which is exactly why the storage exclusion above is not negotiable.
  - **A COMPOSITE USB PARENT IS NOT A GENERIC-DRIVER DEVICE, and a package that binds to its CHILD interface is a success** (2026-08-06, field log + screenshot). `usb.inf`/usbccgp is the *correct* driver for a multi-function USB parent — its job is to split the device into `&MI_00`, `&MI_01`… children, and the vendor driver goes on those children, which Windows enumerates (and this program searches) separately. Treating the parent as "stuck on a Windows generic driver" produced two field symptoms in one run: the Razer parent (`USB\VID_1532&PID_00B9`) got a catalog package that staged **200 unused INFs** (all of which then had to be unstaged) and was reported as *"az eszköz a Windows driverén maradt (usb.inf) - nem valódi csere"* — a verdict the parent can never pass; and the Creative parent (`USB\VID_041E&PID_3274`) was re-offered **pre-ticked** on every scan even though its package was already installed on the child (`pnputil` said so in as many words: `up-to-date on device: USB\VID_041E&PID_3274&MI_00\…`). Two changes, and note what they are *not*: `wu_core.is_composite_parent` makes such a parent **not a generic-replace candidate** (the device stays in every search — the deep scan still queries the catalog for it — we simply stop claiming its driver needs replacing, and the ordinary version gate then declines the no-op offer), and the post-install bind check accepts a bind **anywhere in the device's own family** (`wu_core.pnputil_bound_devices` + `package_bound_to_device_family`, matching the `USB\VID_xxxx&PID_yyyy` / `PCI\VEN_xxxx&DEV_xxxx` stem against the instances pnputil names). This is not a device filter and must not become one — see the governing rule.

### AutoFix extras & reporting

- **AutoFix extras on top of the shared core (`app/gui/autofix.py`)**: per-UID `attempt_counts` allows ONE retry of a failed install before permanent exclusion (successes drop out server-side via `IsInstalled=0`; the old first-offer-permanent-exclusion turned one transient download error into a permanently skipped driver); after the WU rounds a **catalog closing round** runs `_catalog_search_collect` + `_install_catalog_sync(task_id='autofix')` on still-error-coded devices (the AutoFix twin of the manual hybrid top-up — before this, a device WU had nothing for stayed broken even when the Catalog had a driver); the no-internet rescue restores the saved Net-driver backup (the nicpack.zip last-resort branch was removed 2026-07-28, explicit user decision — the failure message recommends the shop's USB-RJ45 adapter instead); per-leg install counts persist in `<app_data>\autofix_stats.json` (legs are separate processes) and the final leg emits a summary (`_emit_autofix_summary`: chain-total installs + **packages that never came back** + remaining error-coded devices) then deletes the stats file (leg A also clears it so a stale chain can't pollute the next run). **The "never came back" report (`_emit_missing_packages`) is mandatory, not cosmetic**: AutoFix deletes every third-party package assuming WU will restore whatever the machine really uses, and that assumption is sometimes false — field-seen (2026-07) `vigembus.inf` (ViGEmBus, a driver installed by a *user application*, which WU does not carry at all) and `amdafd.inf` (its `pci\ven_1002&cc_040300` generic class HWID never matches a present device, so the pairing can't find it) both vanished permanently and silently. We cannot reinstall them (there is no source), but the shop must know they're gone. The delete phase writes the pre-deletion package list to `pre_packages` in the stats file, and the comparison keys on the **original** INF name (`oemNN.inf` gets renumbered on reinstall). Read `pre_packages` *before* `_autofix_stats_total_and_clear()` — that call deletes the file. **The report splits into three buckets, and that split is load-bearing**: a bare "this INF name is gone" list misleads badly — measured 2026-07-25 it flagged 12 packages, of which `nv_dispi.inf` was simply superseded by `nvle.inf` (same vendor, same class) and 11 Razer packages were replaced by one generic `rz00b9dev.inf` while the mouse stayed plugged in and working. So: *lecserélődött* (same provider **and** class present now), *a gyártó másik csomagja* (same provider, other class), and only the rest is a real disappearance — which is the only bucket that gets the "plug the device in / reinstall the program" advice. **The two benign buckets are screen-capped at 15 items** (`BUCKET_SCREEN_MAX`, 2026-07-28): the first post-bloat run classified 175 ex-Razer INFs as *lecserélődött* and the item wall scrolled every useful summary line off screen; above the cap one line says how many more there are and the full name list goes into the log (the delete phase names every package anyway). The real-disappearance bucket is deliberately never capped — that is the actionable list.
  - **Deletion preview with per-package opt-out (`get_autofix_delete_preview`, 2026-08-05, explicit user decision)**: the confirm dialog now has a second column listing **every package that will be deleted**, each with a checkbox (all ticked by default), and — crucially — **what each driver is actually for**. That last part answers a real field failure: a remote-desktop tool's driver ("ninja…") was wiped and afterwards *nobody could tell what it had belonged to*, because an INF filename says nothing. `wu_core.collect_driver_usage` maps published INF → the **names of the present devices** using it (`Win32_PnPSignedDriver` InfName+DeviceName), and a package with no present device is flagged as "probably installed by an application" — which is exactly the ninja case. **This does NOT reintroduce automatic selective deletion** (see the rule below, which stands): every exception here is named on screen and ticked off by a human, the same principle as the printer checkbox, just per package. Rows are locked (greyed, not clickable) when the program would keep them anyway: `boot` **always**, `printer` while the printer toggle is on, `wifi` while Wi-Fi mode is on — and the left-hand toggles re-render the list live. The grouping is computed **by the same functions the delete phase uses** (`_collect_printer_protection`, `collect_wifi_protection`, `_is_boot_path_protected` — note the last one is a 3-tuple + a `detected=False` fail-safe branch, so call the shared predicate instead of testing INF membership yourself; the offline test caught exactly that mistake). If preview and reality ran on separate logic, the preview would eventually lie to the technician. **The boot lock has TWO different causes and the UI must say which one** (2026-08-05, first question the panel got asked in the field): with the chain resolved, the package is provably on the system disk's path (dev box: `disk.inf` → **`oem11.inf`/`iaahcic.inf`, service `iaStorA`** → `pci.inf` → `acpi.inf` → `hal.inf` — i.e. an Intel RST controller carrying the boot volume, the exact `INACCESSIBLE_BOOT_DEVICE` case); with the chain **un**resolved, the fail-safe protects every `BOOT_FALLBACK_PROTECT_CLASSES` package instead, and claiming "it is on the boot path" would assert more than we know — the same overreach as the retracted colour-profile inference. `get_autofix_delete_preview` therefore returns `boot_detected` alongside the rows, and the lock text branches on it; the JS default is `true` (the stricter wording) so the pre-fill render can never under-state a lock. Performance: the list renders **instantly** from the already-loaded `drivers` array in the Drivers view (a fresh `dism /Get-Drivers` is 15–50 s and must never block a modal), and the usage/grouping data is filled in asynchronously — same pattern as the Wi-Fi detection. **But "renders instantly" was not enough, and the async fill must GATE the dialog** (2026-08-07, field report from a Dell laptop): until the backend answers, every pre-filled row carries `group: 'normal'`, so **no lock can be drawn at all** — the tech ticked "keep printer drivers", watched nothing happen for ~2 minutes, and reasonably concluded the checkbox was broken. Three changes, and they belong together: (a) the JS **passes its already-loaded driver list** into `get_autofix_delete_preview(known_drivers)`, so the backend skips the `dism` call entirely (that was the bulk of the wait; it still falls back to querying when the list is empty); (b) the four collectors (`collect_driver_usage`, `_collect_printer_protection`, `collect_wifi_protection`, `_collect_boot_path_protection`) run in a 4-thread pool — they are independent and only touch `self._run` (measured on the dev box: **16.0 s serial → 6.7 s parallel**); (c) while the call is in flight the dialog is **honestly disabled** — a sticky "Elemzés folyamatban" strip on the list, all toggles + row checkboxes + Mind/Egyik se disabled, and the **Igen button disabled** and relabelled, because a fix started on ungrouped data would silently ignore the protections. `mcResetLayout` re-enables the button, or a Mégse here would leave every later confirm dialog stuck.
  - **The Wi-Fi rows follow the REBUILD switch, not just the Wi-Fi switch** (explicit user decision, 2026-08-07): Wi-Fi mode on + rebuild on → the row is locked **and ticked** (it really is deleted, then immediately restored from the backup — showing it unticked would be a lie); Wi-Fi mode on + rebuild off → locked, **unticked** (kept untouched); Wi-Fi mode off → not locked at all, deleted with everything else. Previously the rebuild switch had **no effect whatsoever** on the list, which is what the field report called out. `delRowLock` (a why-string) was therefore replaced by `delRowForce` returning `{on, why}` — a lock now also carries *which way* it is forced, and `setAllDeletePreview`/the `keepPackages` collection both key off it. Offline-tested in Node against a DOM stub (20 assertions: all four switch combinations, the counter, both boot-lock wordings, the loading gate, manual exceptions). The manual exceptions travel to the deleting leg through `autofix_stats.json` (`keep_packages`, written **after** leg A's `_autofix_stats_clear()`), and are re-validated there against the **original** INF name so a renumbered `oemNN.inf` can never shield a different package. Every exception is logged by name.
  - **Deleting EVERY third-party package is the whole point of AutoFix — never "improve" it into selective deletion** (explicit user decision, 2026-07). This is a repair-shop tool run on machines whose drivers are already bad; the design is *strip everything, let WU put back clean current drivers*. So a package disappearing is NOT a bug to fix by protecting the package, and no "skip drivers that look important / are in use / have no WU match" filter may be added. The only opt-out is the printer checkbox (see Printer protection 2.0), which the user drives. The single legitimate follow-up is **reporting**: `_emit_missing_packages` must state the two real causes — (1) **the device was not plugged in during the fix**, so WU could not offer a driver for it (WU and `_match_wu_updates_to_devices` only ever match *present* hardware: a disconnected BT dongle / controller / printer loses its driver and cannot get it back automatically), (2) it was an application-installed driver WU never carries — and must tell the shop the actual remedy: plug the device in, then run a scan in "Driver Keresés és Telepítés". Field-confirmed on the 2026-07-24 run (`rtkfilter.inf` Realtek Bluetooth + `sie_usb_device.inf` Sony, both for unplugged devices); the old wording blamed WU ("a Windows Update nem ismeri") and offered only "reinstall the program", which is wrong for case (1). **There is a THIRD cause, and since 2026-08-06 it is its own bucket: the device is still in the machine, and no source has a package for it.** Field case: `ag276qzd2.inf` (AOC AG276QZD2 monitor) landed in the "never came back" list with the *plug it in / reinstall the program* advice, while the very same monitor appears 20 lines lower in `_emit_driver_health` as present and running on `monitor.inf` — it had been plugged in the whole time, and `MONITOR\AOCA610` returned **zero** catalog rows on all four queries. The two lists contradicted each other on the same device. The delete phase therefore records, per package, the **names of the devices that were using it** (`wu_core.collect_driver_usage`, one WMI call, stored in `pre_packages[].devices`), and the report checks those names against the machine's current device list: still present → *"NEM találtunk pótlást, pedig az eszköz A GÉPBEN VAN"* + the device's name + the vendor-download-page remedy (for a monitor that also brings back the factory ICC profile); not present → the old, correct wording. With no `devices` data (a chain started before this change) it degrades to the old report.
  - **Failed (non-stalled) package deletions are reported too** (`_delete_third_party_sync` collects them via `drivers_core.delete_succeeded` and lists them): a `pnputil` refusal like "at least one device is currently using the specified INF" used to be dropped on the floor while the phase still printed a clean `✅ Driverek eltávolítva` — the same silent-false-success class of bug as the Build 218 `0xC0000142` case. It is not an error (the chain carries on), it just must not be invisible.
  - **The closing summary also reports what stayed on a Windows inbox driver** (`_emit_driver_health`, 2026-07): the quietest failure mode is a device that ends the chain error-code-free on Microsoft's generic driver because neither WU nor the Catalog had a vendor package (field-measured on an ASRock B450M: onboard audio + LAN both generic after a flawless chain). **The report's filter is deliberately NARROWER than the search's, and they are now separate functions** — do not re-unify them. Until 2026-07-28 the list was "exactly `is_generic_replace_candidate`", which was correct while that predicate still carried a class whitelist + bus-INF blocklist + vendor-HWID test. That same day those three filters were removed *from the search* (the governing rule: ask the Catalog about everything), and the report — sharing the predicate — silently inherited the widening: the field run printed **`🏭 87 eszköz Windows-alapdriveren maradt`** (PCI bridges, DMA controller, system timer, WAN Miniports) against only `ℹ️ További 5 eszköz … és ez így HELYES` — the exact inversion of what is useful, and the same 42-of-which-40-noise failure an earlier measurement had already shown. The two questions genuinely differ: *"is it worth asking the Catalog?"* is yes for everything (a miss costs seconds), while *"does the tech have a TODO here?"* is only yes where a vendor driver can exist at all. `_health_report_worth_listing` (in `app/gui/autofix.py`, report-only) applies two tests: `is_specific_hwid` (a type-coded ID like `ACPI\VEN_PNP&DEV_0100` has no vendor package by construction) and `wu_core.HEALTH_REPORT_SKIP_INFS` (Windows bus/input/software-device INFs). **`hdaudio.inf` and `monitor.inf` are deliberately NOT in that skip list** — a chip on Microsoft's generic audio driver and a "Generic PnP Monitor" are the two most actionable findings the report exists for (the monitor case doubly so: the vendor monitor INF is what supplies the factory ICC profile that `colorprofile_core`'s reset assumes is there). Measured live on the dev machine after the fix: 98 devices → 71 on inbox drivers → **3 listed**, all three real (BenQ monitor on `monitor.inf`, HP LaserJet on `wsdprint.inf`, Realtek ALC221 on `hdaudio.inf`). Everything else on an inbox driver is reported as a single count.
  - **ONE CATALOG PACKAGE PER DEVICE PER CHAIN — `catalog_done` in `autofix_stats.json`, or the chain loops until the leg cap** (2026-08-25, field-proven, ASUS Vivobook Go E1504FA, Build 272). The chain ran all **10** legs and burned ~40 minutes; after leg 2 every single leg installed exactly **one** package: an **AMD Radeon catalog cab, 762 MB, re-downloaded eight times** (~6 GB). Every individual step was behaving correctly, which is why it was invisible: (1) the package installed — `Added driver packages: 3` — so the round counted 1 install and `should_chain` fired; (2) `_cleanup_unused_staged_infs` correctly unstaged 3 of its 5 INFs, since no present device used them; (3) the **next** leg therefore saw the package as not installed again — the parent device's version was unchanged because the driver had bound to the **child interface** (`a csomag az eszköz gyerek-interfészére kötött rá (a szülő marad oem8.inf, ez így helyes)`) — and re-downloaded it; (4) back to step 1. **`catalog_no_bind` could not catch this**, because it is not a no-bind case: the package installed *and* bound. The fix records every catalog item the chain **attempts** (not just the ones that fail) into `catalog_done`, and later legs skip those keys. It is written **before** `_install_catalog_sync` runs, so a crash or reboot mid-install cannot restart the same multi-hundred-MB download. Safe by construction: within one chain, re-installing the same package on the same device is redundant by definition — either it worked, or it did not bind and `catalog_no_bind` owns that case. The list dies with the stats file, so a later fix tries again. **The OEM round needed the identical guard and did not get it at the same time — `oem_done`, added 2026-08-26 after the same loop reappeared there** (HP EliteDesk 800 G1, Build 277): the whole-machine `HP driver pack sp92706` was re-downloaded and re-installed on every leg (16:14, 16:21, 16:26, 16:31, ~7 minutes each) until the technician aborted the chain. `_oem_already_current` structurally cannot catch this case: a whole-machine pack has **no device** (`dev is None`), so that function returns "install it" by definition, and the pack's version cannot be compared against any single device's driver version. When a per-chain guard is added to one install path, check every other install path for the same hole.
  - **RE-ENUMERATION ONLY WORKS WITH A REBOOT — learned the hard way, on customer machines** (`app/gui/rebind.py`, 2026-08-25). The repair for a device stuck on an inbox driver is to remove its PnP node so Windows re-selects a driver from scratch. Three field runs taught three things, in order:
    - **`pnputil /remove-device` + `/scan-devices` WITHOUT a reboot repairs nothing, and leaves the machine worse.** Measured (Build 273, T580): 8 devices removed, **0** rebound, and the technician took delivery of a laptop whose touchpad was now broken *by the repair button*. The node is gone and the running PnP stack does not rebuild the binding. `_rebind_device` therefore returns **`'fixed' | 'needs_reboot' | 'failed'`**, the manual button offers the restart and states plainly that the affected devices do not work until then, and the AutoFix runs the repair **before its settle reboot** so the chain's own restart completes it. This is the whole mechanism behind the accidental discovery that started it: the technician pulled the SSD, put it back, and the touchpad worked — the cure was not the removal, it was the **boot that followed**.
    - **Do not try to work out which package the device needs — let Windows decide.** The first implementation matched staged DriverStore INFs to devices by hardware ID and got it wrong in both directions, verified on the machine's own disk: `extract_inf_hardware_ids` keeps only IDs containing an underscore, so `ACPI\LEN009B` is invisible and **the Synaptics package could never be matched to the very touchpad the feature exists for**, while the loose `_hwid_matches` paired `intelgnssdriver.inf` (Intel GPS) and `appleusbvhci.inf` (Apple USB) onto a generic USB composite device. Removing the node and rebooting needs no package guess at all, and cannot end worse than it started: if nothing better exists, Windows re-binds the same inbox driver.
    - **The candidate set is EVERYTHING on an inbox driver except storage, firmware and the boot chain — the class WHITELIST was reversed on 2026-08-25 (explicit user decision) and must not be reinstated.** The user's instruction, verbatim: *"mindent rugjon meg ami nem boot kritikus, nyilvan a tarolo es firmware ne legyen megrugva, de ezen kívül MINDEN meg lehet rúgva… ha van gyári driver akkor inkább az legyen fent mint az inbox, kivéve a vezérlő és a firmware"*. The whitelist (`REBIND_ALLOWED_CLASSES`: Mouse/HIDClass/Keyboard, Media, Net/Bluetooth, Camera/Image/Biometric, SmartCardReader, Monitor) repeated the mistake this file already records for the generic-replace class lists: a **video card** stuck on the Microsoft Basic Display Adapter, a card reader, or a chipset node did not get the vendor driver **already sitting on the machine**, purely because its class was not listed. What replaced it (`REBIND_BLOCKED_CLASSES` = `STORAGE_RISK_CLASSES | FIRMWARE_RISK_CLASSES`) plus two non-class guards:
      - **The system disk's whole device chain is excluded by INSTANCE ID, not by class** (`_rebind_boot_chain_ids`, reusing the delete phase's `_collect_boot_path_protection`). The class list cannot cover this: measured on the dev box the chain is `WD Blue SN580 [DiskDrive] → NVMe controller [SCSIAdapter] → **PCI Express Root Port [System]** → Root Complex [System] → ACPI [System]`, i.e. the port carrying the boot NVMe is `System` class and the class rule would happily rip it out of a running system. If the chain cannot be resolved, `_rebind_boot_chain_ids` returns `None` and the candidate filter falls back to skipping `REBIND_BOOT_FALLBACK_CLASSES` (`SYSTEM`, `COMPUTER`) — same fail-safe reasoning as `BOOT_FALLBACK_PROTECT_CLASSES`.
      - **`is_specific_hwid` stays.** A type-coded device (system timer, PCI bridge) cannot have a vendor package by construction, so re-enumerating it is guaranteed to return the same inbox driver — pure cost, no gain. This is not a device filter in the search sense; nothing is being hidden.
      - The Build-274 measurement that justified the whitelist is still **true** and is now an accepted trade, not a bug: the round does select the TPM, the USB host controller, the root hubs and PCIe ports. Removing a TPM *node* is not `tpm.msc → Clear` (BitLocker keys are untouched); removing the USB controller node, however, **kills the keyboard and mouse until the reboot** — which is why the manual button now takes its consent **up front** and then **reboots by itself** after a 10 s countdown (`_rebind_finish_reboot`). The old flow asked "shall I reboot?" *after* the sweep, i.e. exactly when the technician could no longer click anything.
      - Measured after the change (dev box, ASRock B450M Pro4): **36 → 62 candidates**, 6 boot-chain devices excluded, and the newly reachable ones include the GPU/audio-controller/card-reader class of device the whitelist had been hiding. INF reads are cached per package (`_rebind_pkg_ids`) — without it the widened set would re-read the same few INFs 62× (~1200 file reads).
    - **THE SWEEP RUNS AT THE END OF THE CHAIN, AND THE CHAIN DOES NOT CLOSE UNTIL IT HAS** (`_autofix_closing_rebind`, `rebind_done` in `autofix_stats.json`, explicit user decision 2026-08-25: *"a lánc végén telibe ez fusson le és csak akkor legyen vége a láncnak ha ez lefutott… amikor visszajovok a kavezasrol minden hibatlanul mukodjon"*). Placement is load-bearing: **after** the settle reboot, **before** the closing report — so the sweep decides on a settled machine, the nodes it removes are re-discovered by the reboot it triggers, and `_emit_driver_health` describes the final state instead of a half-dismantled one. It runs the **same** `_rebind_sweep` core as the manual button (two copies would drift, and a field log could not tell which ran). Three behaviours worth keeping: nothing removed ⇒ **no** reboot, the closing continues in the same leg; an exception is swallowed (WARNING) because this is a convenience repair, never a precondition for closing the chain; and a Cancel *after* nodes were removed **still reboots** — a machine handed over without a keyboard is worse than one extra restart — but unregisters the resume task, so the chain stops there.
  - **A 3-CHARACTER ACPI VENDOR CODE IS A SPECIFIC HARDWARE ID — `is_specific_hwid` silently said otherwise, and it hid the exact device this whole feature was written for** (`_HWID_VENDOR_TOKEN_RE`, fixed 2026-08-25). The regex demanded `(?:VEN|VID)_?[0-9A-Z]{4}`, i.e. four characters after the prefix. PCI/USB ids do have four (`VEN_8086`, `VID_046D`), but ACPI devices carry the **three**-character PnP vendor code — and the ThinkPad T580 touchpad is `ACPI\VEN_LEN&DEV_009B`. It therefore failed the test, with three silent consequences: the Microsoft Catalog was **never queried** for it, the closing health report could **never** list it (the "vendor-coded input device" exemption CLAUDE.md documents was dead code for precisely that device), and it could not become a re-enumeration candidate either — the touchpad was invisible to the feature built to fix touchpads. Now `[0-9A-Z]{3,4}(?![0-9A-Z])`; the trailing guard stops a 3-char match from biting into a 4-char code. Regression-tested over 11 specific ids (incl. `ACPI\VEN_MSFT&DEV_0101`, `ACPI\SYNA30A0`, PCI/USB/HDAUDIO/MONITOR forms) and 10 type codes that must still be refused (`ACPI\PNP0501`, `ACPI\VEN_PNP&DEV_0100`, `USB\ROOT_HUB30`, `*PNP0501`…) — the type-code rule from the catalog-key section is unchanged.
  - **The health report's "vendor-coded input" exemption must not fire for USB peripherals** (`_USB_PERIPHERAL_HWID_RE`, 2026-08-25). `_VENDOR_CODED_RE` matches `VID_`, and **every** USB mouse/keyboard/headset carries one, so the exemption pulled the whole HID skip list back into the report: measured on the dev box the closing summary listed **36** devices of which **34** were external peripherals correctly running Microsoft's HID drivers — the "42 rows of which 40 are noise" failure this filter exists to prevent, inverted. Integrated input devices (`ACPI\VEN_LEN&DEV_009B`, `ACPI\SYNA30A0`) are never `VID_`/`PID_` shaped, so the exemption still covers exactly what it was written for. Same measurement also added `hidtelephonydriver.inf` (USB headset volume keys) to `HEALTH_REPORT_SKIP_INFS`. Result on that machine: **36 → 3** rows, all real (onboard audio on `hdaudio.inf`, the monitor on `monitor.inf`, and the NVMe controller the storage checkbox held back).
  - **A DRIVER CAN INSTALL AND STILL FAIL TO START — and the fallback to the inbox driver makes the error code ZERO, so the chain reports success while the device is degraded** (`_fix_driver_regressions`, 2026-08-25, field-proven twice on the same ThinkPad T580). The machine's own `setupapi.dev.log` recorded it verbatim: `Installing best driver (oem19.inf) on device 'ACPI\LEN009B'` → `Strong Name=oem19.inf:…:LENOVO_GROUP53_InterTouch_Win8_Inst:19.3.4.228` → `Start: ACPI\LEN009B` → `Device 'ACPI\LEN009B' not started: Device has problem: 0x0a (CM_PROB_FAILED_START)`. Windows then fell back to the generic `msmouse.inf` PS/2 path, which starts perfectly — so `ConfigManagerErrorCode` was 0, the closing report printed `✅ Nem maradt hibakódos eszköz a rendszerben!`, and the technician got a laptop whose touchpad stuttered, jumped and lost tracking (and, the day before, whose UltraNav buttons did nothing). **The error code can never catch this class of failure**, because the fallback is by definition healthy. Three rules came out of it:
    - **The delete phase snapshots every device that runs on a VENDOR driver (`pre_device_drivers` in `autofix_stats.json`), and the final leg compares.** A device that was on a vendor driver before and is on an inbox one after is a **regression**, reported loudly and repaired — no version arithmetic, no error codes, just before-vs-after.
    - **Repair escalates, and the second step is the one that actually works: re-enumerate the device** (`pnputil /remove-device` + `/scan-devices`, falling back to `Disable-PnpDevice`/`Enable-PnpDevice` below Win10 1903). Re-running `pnputil /add-driver …/install` alone often changes nothing, because Windows considers the current driver adequate; removing the device node forces driver selection **from scratch**. This is the software equivalent of what the technician did by accident — pulling the SSD and putting it back, after which the Synaptics driver bound correctly and the touchpad worked. **Never re-enumerate a storage device** (`STORAGE_RISK_CLASSES`): a boot-path node vanishing for a few seconds can take the running system with it, while an input device disappearing briefly is harmless in an unattended chain.
    - This is **not** the banned "back up the old driver and restore it" pattern. The package being re-bound is the one *this chain just installed*, sitting staged in the DriverStore (`wu_core.driverstore_package_inf`); we are finishing an install that failed on a live system, not preserving the machine's pre-fix state.
    - Related reporting fix: `msmouse.inf`/`input.inf` are in `HEALTH_REPORT_SKIP_INFS`, so the degraded touchpad was not merely unreported — the summary actively said *"billentyűzet/egér - ezekhez gyári driver nem is létezik"*, which is false for a precision touchpad whose vendor package sits in Lenovo's own catalog. `_health_report_worth_listing` now exempts **vendor-coded** input devices (`ACPI\VEN_LEN&DEV_009B`) from that skip list; a plain USB mouse on `msmouse.inf` is still correctly silent.
  - **THE CHAIN ENDS WITH A SETTLE REBOOT — the closing cleanup, health report and summary must run on a machine that has booted since the last install** (2026-08-25, field case). Until this, the last install leg finished *without* rebooting: it printed "kész", and the machine was handed over in whatever half-settled state the final round left it — including anything the **catalog closing round installed in that same leg**, whose `3010`/reboot-pending results never got their boot. The `_emit_driver_health` report was computed in that same unsettled state, so it could warn about devices that were about to be fine. Field case (ThinkPad T580, 2026-08-24): the chain provably installed the correct Lenovo/Synaptics UltraNav driver — the machine's own `setupapi.dev.log` records `Strong Name=oem17.inf:…:LENOVO_GROUP53_InterTouch_Win8_Inst:19.3.4.228:acpi\len009b` followed by `Start:` — yet the technician took delivery of a machine whose trackpad buttons did not work and reasonably concluded the program had broken it. **The same machine, untouched, worked after a later boot.** So: when the round would close the chain, it instead sets `settle_done` in `autofix_stats.json`, schedules one more `--resume-autofix` and reboots; the returning leg sees the flag and goes straight to the closing. Exactly **one** extra reboot is possible — the flag lives in the stats file, which survives reboots and is cleared both at chain start (`_autofix_stats_clear`) and at chain end (`_autofix_stats_total_and_clear`). Do not "optimize" this away to save a minute: without it the chain can hand back a machine that is one boot short of correct, which is precisely what the [acceptance criterion](#the-acceptance-criterion-for-the-one-click-fix-press-it-walk-away-come-back-to-a-finished-machine) forbids.
  - **`🎉 MINDEN LÉPÉS KÉSZ!` is only emitted when no further leg is chained** (it now reads `EZ A KÖR KÉSZ!`): it used to print at the end of every install leg, immediately followed by a reboot, which reads as "finished" plus an unexplained restart.

### Kijelző & Színkezelés view (`app/gui/display.py` + `app/display_core.py`)

GUI-only view added 2026-07-31 on explicit user request, because Windows 11 scatters these settings across three places (Settings > Display > HDR, the legacy Color Management control panel, the Store-only HDR Calibration app) and none of them answers the two questions the shop actually has: *what can this panel really do*, and *is the machine's colour management even intact*. Every API below was probed on real hardware before a line of the view was written (AOC AG276QZD2 QD-OLED, DisplayPort, RTX 3060) — the captured sample lives in the `display_core.py` module docstring, and it is what the parsers were built against.

- **HDR is toggled through DisplayConfig, and the RETURN CODE IS NOT THE SUCCESS TEST — the read-back is.** `set_hdr` tries `DISPLAYCONFIG_DEVICE_INFO_SET_HDR_STATE` (16, Win11 24H2+) first and falls back to the older `SET_ADVANCED_COLOR_STATE` (10) **only if the state did not actually change**, because the old call can enable WCG instead of HDR. Measured live: type 16 flipped the panel to `activeColorMode=2`, 8→10 bits, and back again cleanly. The struct `size` fields must match Windows' definitions exactly — a wrong size makes `DisplayConfigSetDeviceInfo` return 0 while doing nothing, which is exactly the silent-false-success class this project keeps getting bitten by. That is why the verdict is always "did the re-read change?".
- **Read both advanced-colour APIs, prefer the new one, never rely on only one.** `GET_ADVANCED_COLOR_INFO` (9) exists on every Win10/11 and gives supported/enabled/bits/encoding; `GET_ADVANCED_COLOR_INFO_2` (15) exists from Win11 24H2 and additionally separates **HDR from WCG** (`hdrSupported`/`hdrUserEnabled`/`wcgSupported`/`wcgUserEnabled`) and reports the active colour mode. `read_hdr_state` fills from the old one and then overwrites with the new one when available, so an older Windows still renders a complete card instead of an empty one.
- **The nit values come from the EDID's CTA-861 HDR Static Metadata block, and they are the MANUFACTURER'S CLAIM, not a measurement** — the UI says so in as many words. Software cannot measure luminance without a colorimeter, and promising otherwise would be the one thing a shop tool must not do. Decoding is `nits = 50 * 2^(raw/32)` for peak and frame-average; the minimum is derived from the peak. Measured on the dev panel: peak 1015, frame-average 254, black 0.0006 nit — textbook QD-OLED.
- **A monitor has SEVERAL EDID entries in the registry and only one of them is the real one.** Live measurement found three `DISPLAY\AOCA610\…` instances for one physical panel: two 128-byte stubs with no CTA extension (⇒ no HDR data at all) and one 384-byte full EDID. `collect_edids` therefore keeps the **longest** EDID per instance key, and `match_edid` resolves the DisplayConfig device path (`\\?\DISPLAY#AOCA610#5&…&UID28931#{guid}`) to `AOCA610\5&…&UID28931`, falling back to the model's largest EDID when the exact instance is absent. Matching on the model alone, or taking the first entry found, would silently report "this monitor has no HDR data" on a monitor that does.
- ****The calibration switch lives in `ICM\Calibration`, not in the `ICM` root** — my first cut of `calibration_management()` read the root and therefore returned `None` on a machine where the value was sitting at 0 one key deeper, i.e. it was blind to precisely the state the view exists to surface (`colorprofile_core` has always written the correct path). It now reads **both** locations and a 0 anywhere wins over a 1 elsewhere: a missing value must never be reported as "fine" while the other location says otherwise. `set_calibration_management(enabled)` writes both values, and **both directions are legitimate** (explicit user decision): 1 is the Windows default and merely *permits* a profile's gamma curve to load (it enables nothing by itself), while 0 is a deliberate lock used on a wide-gamut OLED whose owner wants the native, unmanaged picture and wants to be sure nothing — an update, a vendor tool, another app — can load a gamma ramp behind their back. The view therefore offers a toggle with the current state shown, not a one-way "repair" button.
- `registered_profiles_report` exists because of a real, invisible breakage found on the dev box during this work.** `HKCU\…\ICM\RegisteredProfiles\sRGB` pointed at `srgb_to_gamma2p2_300_mhc2.icm`, a file some third-party HDR tool had left registered and then deleted. Consequence: **every** `mscms` call on that machine returned `ERROR_FILE_NOT_FOUND`, and no Windows UI mentions it anywhere. The view detects it, names the exact registry values, and `repair_registered_profiles` removes **only** entries whose file is provably missing (intact ones are never touched, every removal is logged by name at WARNING first). Windows falls back to its own built-in default, so the removal is the repair, not damage.
- **The view reports the MONITOR's driver (`monitor_driver`, `generic_monitor`), because "what is the factory state?" has no single answer.** On a monitor running the generic `monitor.inf` there is no factory ICC at all, so a linear ramp genuinely *is* the out-of-box state (measured: the dev box's AOC is `Generic PnP Monitor`). On a monitor that has a vendor INF, that INF installs **and associates** a factory ICC — there the out-of-box state is *not* linear, and a chain that stripped the vendor monitor package took the profile with it. Note this second path is **not** `colorprofile_core`: the ordinary third-party driver deletion removes the monitor's `oemNN.inf` too, and that is correct and must stay. The remedy is to get the vendor monitor driver back (Monitor is in the driver search since 2026-07-27), not to weaken the deletion.
- **The GPU gamma ramp is read and drawn, because it is the only DIRECT fact about the colour path.** `read_gamma_ramp` opens a DC on the specific display (`CreateDCW` with the GDI name, not the desktop DC — on a multi-monitor box each output can carry a different ramp) and calls `GetDeviceGammaRamp`. A linear ramp proves nothing is correcting the signal, whatever the profile list says; a modified one proves something *is*, even when no association is visible. The view therefore shows a LINEÁRIS/MÓDOSÍTVA badge, the max deviation from linear, and a 17-point SVG curve per channel against a dashed reference — a badge alone would not say *which way* it deviates. `GAMMA_LINEAR_TOLERANCE` is 64/65535 (~0.1%), not 0, because driver rounding makes an untouched ramp jitter by a few units; measured on the dev box an untouched ramp gives exactly 0. This is also what settles "did my change take effect?" questions: disabling calibration management does not clear an already-loaded ramp — that survives until the next logon/reboot, and the box is where the technician sees it actually go linear.
- **Count the association VALUES, not the keys** — the same lesson `colorprofile_core` already records: the `ProfileAssociations\Display\{GUID}\NNNN` subkeys are mostly empty containers and the profile is a *value* inside them. `_read_assoc_branch` walks down to the values and handles both `REG_SZ` and `REG_MULTI_SZ`.
- **Profile association is written STRAIGHT TO THE REGISTRY, never through mscms** (solved 2026-07-31 after three failed attempts). Every association API is a silent no-op on this Windows 11: `WcsAssociateColorProfileWithDevice` returns TRUE and writes nothing (elevated and not, system and per-user scope), the matching disassociate then reports `ERROR_PROFILE_NOT_ASSOCIATED_WITH_DEVICE`, and the legacy `AssociateColorProfileWithDeviceW` returns FALSE with no error set. Removing the stale `RegisteredProfiles` entry did **not** change this — that hypothesis was measured and killed. What works: write `ICMProfile` (REG_MULTI_SZ) + `UsePerUserProfiles`=1 to `HKCU\…\ICM\ProfileAssociations\Display\{class-GUID}\{instance}`, where both trailing components come straight out of the monitor's `DeviceID` (`MONITOR\AOCA610\{4d36e96e-…} 3`). Verified end to end: Windows' own Color Management control panel immediately showed the profile with **(default)** and "Use my settings for this device" ticked. `InstallColorProfileW` is the one mscms call that does work, so profile *installation* goes through it. If anyone ever moves this back to the API, the acceptance test is exactly that control-panel check — a TRUE return proves nothing here.
- **Still NOT in this view:** ICC generation and the guided nit test.
- The Windows-side tools (`colorcpl`, `dccw`, `ms-settings:display-hdr`, the Store HDR Calibration app) are reachable as **link-outs**, and `open_windows_color_tool` only ever runs commands from the `WINDOWS_COLOR_TOOLS` table — the key coming from JS is looked up, never executed, so the frontend cannot be talked into launching an arbitrary program.

### TÁROLÓ ÉS FIRMWARE: VÉGLEGESEN TILTVA, NINCS HOZZÁ KAPCSOLÓ (2026-09-02)

**Explicit user decision:** *„tüntesd el innen a tároló vezérlők illetve a firmware frissítések kapcsolót, ne lehessen bekapcsolni sose… ahogy az autofixnél úgy a manual driver scannél is! inkább ne tudják bekapcsolni az ügyfelek mert abbol sok baj lehet"*. A 2026-07-27/28-i „két checkbox, alapból KI" megoldás ezzel **visszavonva** — a kapcsolók eltűntek, a mögöttes szűrők változatlanok.

- **A KÉT KOCKÁZAT, AMIÉRT NEM VOLT TARTHATÓ EGY BEKAPCSOLHATÓ KAPCSOLÓ.** Rossz tároló-driver után a Windows **el sem indul** (`INACCESSIBLE_BOOT_DEVICE`), és azt már csak helyreállító adathordozóról lehet javítani — miközben az AutoFix **felügyelet nélkül** fut, tehát a hiba akkor derül ki, amikor a technikus visszaér a kávézásból; a firmware-írás pedig visszafordíthatatlan (sem visszaállítási pont, sem Windows-újratelepítés nem hozza vissza), és egy megszakadt flash hardveresen teszi tönkre az eszközt. Egy alapból kikapcsolt kapcsoló ezekre nem elég védelem: elég egyszer bekapcsolva hagyni.
- **NÉGY HELYEN KELLETT KIVENNI, ÉS EBBŐL KETTŐT KÖNNYŰ ELFELEJTENI.** (a) `ui.html` AutoFix-dialógus (`mc-allow-storage`/`mc-allow-firmware` + a `showAutoFixConfirm` olvasásai, a `delSetLoading` id-listája, a `startAutoFix` változói); (b) `ui.html` kézi szken (`hw-allow-storage`/`hw-allow-firmware`, a nézetbe lépéskori visszaállító ciklus, a `start_hw_scan` hívás argumentumai); (c) **`app/cli/menu.py` — a CLI mindkét helyen KÉRDEZTE** (`_hwscan_flow` és az AutoFix-folyamat), és mivel a CLI 2026-08-29 óta teljes értékű felület, enélkül a kapcsoló csak a grafikus felületről tűnt volna el; (d) **`app/gui/base.py` `--allow-storage-drivers` / `--allow-firmware` argv-kapcsolói**, amiket egy régi, még ki nem futott ütemezett feladat hordozhat.
- **A ZÁRÁS EGY KÖZÖS KAPUN VAN, NEM A HÍVÁSI HELYEKEN.** `run_autofix` workerében az `if/else` **után** (tehát a resume-ág és a dialógus-ág után egyaránt), illetve `start_hw_scan` elején egyetlen `allow_storage = False` / `allow_fw = False` pár. Ez az egy pont semlegesíti a JS-paramétert, a CLI-hívást és a régi ütemezett feladat argumentumát is — szemben azzal, ha hívási helyenként írnánk át, ahol a projekt legrégebbi visszatérő hibája (egy példány lemarad) azonnal visszatérne. Ha nem-alapértelmezett érték érkezik, azt **WARNING-gal kilogozzuk**: az onnantól hívási hiba, nem felhasználói döntés.
- **A PARAMÉTEREK ÉS A TELJES LOGIKA SZÁNDÉKOSAN A HELYÜKÖN MARADTAK** (explicit user decision: *„ha pedig a jövőben akarnék ezekkel bármit csinalni akkor ott lesz a kod csak vissza kell rakni… és akkor nem gubaszolodik semmi össze a kódban"*). A `wu_core` szűrői (`filter_autofix_risky_devices`, `filter_firmware_updates`, `is_generic_replace_candidate`, `deep_catalog_candidates`), a piros `⛔ KOCKÁZATOS` jelölés és a lánc-flagek érintetlenek. Újra kapcsolhatóvá tétel = a két értékadás törlése + a felületi kapcsolók visszatétele; semmit nem kell újraírni.
- **A CSOMAG-SZINTŰ FIRMWARE-SZŰRÉS TOVÁBBRA IS ÉLŐ KÓD, ne töröld „halottként".** Egy SSD-firmware a **tárolóvezérlőhöz**, egy dokkoló-firmware egy **USB-eszközhöz** párosul — az eszköz-osztály szűrője tehát nem fogja meg. `is_firmware_update` + `filter_firmware_updates` ezért ugyanúgy kell, és a `FIRMWARE_CLASS_LABEL` jelölés is megjelenhet egy nem-firmware-osztályú eszköz során.
- **A KÉPERNYŐN MEGJELENŐ MAGYARÁZATOT IS ÁT KELLETT ÍRNI, KÜLÖNBEN HAZUDIK.** A `skipped_risky` sor 2026-09-02-ig azt mondta: *„kapcsold be a pipát, ha ezekre is keressen"* — a pipa azóta nem létezik, tehát az útmutatás nem létező felületre küldene. Az új szöveg kimondja, hogy ez szándékos, és megadja a valódi teendőt (gyártói oldal, kézzel). Ugyanez a kézi szken nézetében egy `hw-risk-note` magyarázó sor, a két csúszka helyén: a *„miért nem talált az SSD-mhez drivert?"* kérdésre a felületen is kell válasz.

### GYORS MÓD — a Microsoft Update Catalog kikapcsolható (2026-09-02)

**Explicit user decision:** *„legyen egy pipa az autofixnél is illetve a kézi scannél is, hogy keressen-e MS katalógusból… le akarom rövidíteni az időt"*. Két jelölőnégyzet, **mindkettő alapból BE**: `mc-use-catalog` (AutoFix dialógus) és `hw-use-catalog` (kézi szken). A CLI ugyanezt kérdezi (`use_catalog`).
